# Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization

\name​Daniel Golovin\emailantispam.golovin@gmail.com
Affiliation: \addrCalifornia Institute of Technology
Affiliation: Pasadena, CA 91125, USA
   \nameAndreas Krause\emailantispam.krausea@ethz.ch
Affiliation: \addrETH Zurich
Affiliation: 8092 Zurich, Switzerland

###### Abstract

Many problems in artificial intelligence require adaptively making a sequence of decisions with uncertain outcomes under partial observability. Solving such stochastic optimization problems is a fundamental but notoriously difficult challenge. In this paper, we introduce the concept of *adaptive submodularity*, generalizing submodular set functions to adaptive policies. We prove that if a problem satisfies this property, a simple adaptive greedy algorithm is guaranteed to be competitive with the optimal policy. In addition to providing performance guarantees for both stochastic maximization and coverage, adaptive submodularity can be exploited to drastically speed up the greedy algorithm by using lazy evaluations. We illustrate the usefulness of the concept by giving several examples of adaptive submodular objectives arising in diverse AI applications including management of sensing resources, viral marketing and active learning. Proving adaptive submodularity for these problems allows us to recover existing results in these applications as special cases, improve approximation guarantees and handle natural generalizations.

00footnotetext: This work appeared in the Journal of Artificial Intelligence Research ([Golovin and Krause, 2011a](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib23)), and an
eariler extended abstract appeared in the International Conference on Learning Theory ([Golovin and Krause, 2010](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib22)).

Keywords: Adaptive Optimization, Stochastic Optimization, Submodularity, Partial Observability,
Active Learning, Optimal Decision Trees

## 1 Introduction

In many problems arising in artificial intelligence one
needs to adaptively make a sequence of decisions, taking into account
observations about the outcomes of past decisions. Often these
outcomes are uncertain, and one may only know a probability
distribution over them. Finding optimal policies for decision making
in such partially observable stochastic optimization problems is
notoriously intractable (see, e.g., [Littman et al. (1998)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib46)). A fundamental challenge is to identify classes of planning problems for which simple solutions obtain (near-) optimal performance.

In this paper, we
introduce the concept of *adaptive submodularity*, and prove that
if a partially observable stochastic optimization problem satisfies this property, a simple adaptive greedy
algorithm is guaranteed to obtain near-optimal solutions. In fact, under reasonable complexity-theoretic assumptions, no polynomial time algorithm is able to obtain better solutions in general.
Adaptive submodularity generalizes the classical notion of submodularity11 1 For
an extensive treatment of submodularity, see the books
of [Fujishige (2005)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib18) and [Schrijver (2003)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib60)., which has
been successfully used to develop approximation algorithms for a
variety of non-adaptive optimization problems.
Submodularity, informally, is an intuitive notion of diminishing
returns, which states that adding an element to a small set helps
more than adding that same element to a larger (super-) set. A
celebrated result of the work of [Nemhauser et al. (1978)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib54) guarantees that for such
submodular functions, a simple greedy algorithm, which adds the element that maximally increases the objective value, selects a near-optimal set of $k$ elements.
Similarly, it is guaranteed to find a set of near-minimal cost that
achieves a desired quota of utility ([Wolsey, 1982](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib66)), using
near-minimum average time to do so ([Streeter and Golovin, 2008](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib65)).
Besides guaranteeing theoretical performance bounds, submodularity allows us
to speed up algorithms without loss of solution quality by using lazy evaluations ([Minoux, 1978](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib51)),
often leading to performance improvements of several orders of
magnitude ([Leskovec et al., 2007](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib45)). Submodularity has been shown to be very useful in a variety of problems in artificial intelligence ([Krause and Guestrin, 2009b](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib44)).

The challenge in generalizing
submodularity to adaptive planning — where the action taken in each
step depends on information obtained in the previous steps — is that feasible solutions are now
policies (decision trees or conditional plans) instead of subsets. We propose a natural
generalization of the diminishing returns property for adaptive
problems, which reduces to the classical characterization of submodular set
functions for deterministic distributions. We show how these results
of [Nemhauser et al. (1978)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib54), [Wolsey (1982)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib66), [Streeter and Golovin (2008)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib65),
and [Minoux (1978)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib51) generalize to the adaptive setting. Hence,
we demonstrate how adaptive submodular optimization problems enjoy
theoretical and practical benefits similar to those of classical,
nonadaptive submodular problems. We further demonstrate the usefulness
and generality of the concept by showing how it captures known results
in stochastic optimization and active learning as special cases,
admits tighter performance bounds, leads to natural generalizations
and allows us to solve new problems for which no performance guarantees were known.

As a first example, consider the problem of deploying (or controlling) a collection of
sensors to monitor some spatial phenomenon. Each sensor can cover a
region depending on its sensing range. Suppose we would like to find
the best subset of $k$ locations to place the sensors. In this
application, intuitively, adding a sensor helps more if we have placed
few sensors so far and helps less if we have already placed many sensors. We can formalize this diminishing returns property using the notion of submodularity –
the total area covered by the sensors is a submodular function defined
over all sets of locations. [Krause and Guestrin (2007)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib42) show that
many more realistic utility functions in sensor placement (such as the
improvement in prediction accuracy w.r.t. some probabilistic model)
are submodular as well. Now consider the following stochastic variant:
Instead of deploying a fixed set of sensors, we deploy one sensor at a
time. With a certain probability, deployed sensors can fail, and our
goal is to maximize the area covered by the functioning sensors.
Thus, when deploying the next sensor, we need to take into account
which of the sensors we deployed in the past failed. This problem has
been studied by [Asadpour et al. (2008)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib3) for the case where each sensor
fails independently at random. In this paper, we show that the
coverage objective is adaptive submodular, and use this concept to handle
much more general settings (where, e.g., rather than all-or-nothing
failures there are different types of sensor failures of varying severity).
We also consider a related problem where the goal is to place the minimum number of
sensors to achieve the maximum possible sensor coverage (i.e., the coverage
obtained by deploying sensors everywhere), or more generally the
goal may be to achieve any fixed percentage of the maximum possible
sensor coverage.
Under the first goal, the problem is equivalent to one studied by [Goemans and Vondrák (2006)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib21), and
generalizes a problem studied by [Liu et al. (2008)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib47).

As another example, consider a viral marketing problem, where we are
given a social network, and we want to influence as many people as
possible in the network to buy some product. We do that by giving the
product for free to a subset of the people, and hope that they
convince their friends to buy the product as well. Formally, we have
a graph, and each edge $e$ is labeled by a number $0\leq p_{e}\leq 1$. We “influence” a subset of nodes in the graph, and for each influenced node, their neighbors get randomly influenced according to the probability annotated on the edge connecting the nodes. This process repeats until no further node gets influenced. [Kempe et al. (2003)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib39) show that the set function which quantifies the expected number of nodes influenced is submodular. A natural stochastic variant of the problem is where we pick a node, get to see which nodes it influenced, then adaptively pick the next node based on these observations and so on. We show that a large class of such adaptive influence maximization problems satisfies adaptive submodularity.

Our third application is in active learning,
where we are given an unlabeled data set, and we would like to
adaptively pick a small set of examples whose labels imply all other
labels. The same problem arises in automated diagnosis, where we have
hypotheses about the state of the system (e.g., what illness a patient has), and would like to perform tests to identify the correct hypothesis.
In both domains we want to pick examples / tests to shrink the remaining
version space (the set of consistent hypotheses) as quickly as
possible. Here, we show that the reduction in version space
probability mass is
adaptive submodular, and use that observation to prove that the adaptive
greedy algorithm is a near-optimal querying policy.
Our results for active learning and automated diagnosis are also related to recent results of [Guillory and Bilmes (2010)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib33); [Guillory and Bilmes (2011)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib34) who study generalizations of submodular set cover to an interactive setting. In contrast to our approach however, [Guillory and Bilmes](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib33) analyze worst-case costs, and use rather different technical definitions and proof techniques.

We summarize our main contributions below, and provide a more
technical summary in Table [1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S1.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").
At a high level, our main contributions are:

- •

  We consider a particular class of partially observable adaptive stochastic optimization problems, which we prove to be hard to approximate in general.

- •

  We introduce the concept of *adaptive submodularity*, and
  prove that if a problem instance satisfies this property, a simple
  adaptive greedy policy performs near-optimally, for both adaptive
  stochastic maximization and coverage, and also a natural min-sum objective.

- •

  We show how adaptive submodularity can be exploited by allowing the
  use of an accelerated adaptive greedy algorithm using lazy
  evaluations, and how we can obtain data-dependent bounds on the optimum.

- •

  We illustrate adaptive submodularity on several realistic problems,
  including Stochastic Maximum Coverage, Stochastic Submodular Coverage,
  Adaptive Viral Marketing, and
  Active Learning. For these applications, adaptive submodularity allows
  us to recover known results and prove natural generalizations.

### 1.1 Organization

This article is organized as follows.
In §[2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2 "2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (page [2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2 "2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) we set up notation and formally define
the relevant adaptive optimization problems for general objective
functions. *For the reader’s convenience, we have also provided a reference table of
important symbols on page [2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").* In §[3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3 "3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (page [3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3 "3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) we review the classical notion of submodularity and introduce the novel
adaptive submodularity property. In §[4](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S4 "4 The Adaptive Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (page [4](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S4 "4 The Adaptive Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) we introduce
the adaptive greedy policy, as well as an accelerated variant.
In §[5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S5 "5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (page [5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S5 "5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) we discuss the theoretical guarantees
that the adaptive greedy policy enjoys when applied to
problems with adaptive submodular objectives.
Sections [6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") through [9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") provide examples on how to apply the adaptive submodular framework to
various applications, namely Stochastic Submodular Maximization
(§[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), page [6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), Stochastic Submodular Coverage
(§[7](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S7 "7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), page [7](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S7 "7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")),
Adaptive Viral Marketing (§[8](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8 "8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), page [8](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8 "8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), and
Active Learning (§[9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), page [9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")).
In §[10](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10 "10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (page [10](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10 "10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) we report empirical results on two sensor
selection problems.
In §[11](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S11 "11 Adaptivity Gap ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (page [11](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S11 "11 Adaptivity Gap ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) we discuss the adaptivity gap of the problems we
consider, and in §[12](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S12 "12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (page [12](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S12 "12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) we prove hardness results
indicating that problems which are not adaptive submodular can be extremely
inapproximable under reasonable complexity assumptions.
We review related work in §[13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S13 "13 Related Work ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (page [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S13 "13 Related Work ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) and provide concluding remarks in
§[14](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S14 "14 Conclusions ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (page [14](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S14 "14 Conclusions ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")). The Appendix (page [15](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15 "15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) gives details of how to incorporate item
costs and includes all of the proofs omitted from the main text.

## 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization

We start by introducing notation and defining the general class of adaptive optimization problems that we address in this paper. For sake of clarity, we will illustrate our notation using the sensor placement application mentioned in §[1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S1 "1 Introduction ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). We give examples of other applications in §[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), §[7](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S7 "7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), §[8](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8 "8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"),
and §[9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

### 2.1 Items and Realizations

Let $E$ be a finite set
of items (e.g., sensor locations). Each item $e\in E$ is
in a particular (initially unknown) state from a set $O$ of
possible states
(describing whether a sensor
placed at location $e$ would malfunction or not).
We represent the item states using a
function ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}:E\rightarrow O$, called a *realization* (of the states of all items in the ground set).
Thus, we say that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)$ is the state of $e$ under
realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
We use ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}$ to denote a random realization.
We take a Bayesian approach and assume that there is a known prior
probability distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}:=\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\right]$ over realizations (e.g., modeling
that sensors fail independently with failure probability), so that
we can compute posterior distributions22 2 In some situations, we
may not have exact knowledge of the prior ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$. Obtaining
algorithms that are robust to incorrect priors remains an interesting
source of open problems. We briefly discuss some robustness
guarantees of our algorithm in §[4](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S4 "4 The Adaptive Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") on page [4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S4.SS3 "4.3 Robustness & Approximate Greedy Selection ‣ 4 The Adaptive Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")..
We will consider problems where we sequentially pick an item$e\in E$, get to see its state ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)$, pick the
next item, get to see its state, and so on (e.g., place a sensor, see whether it fails, and so on). After each pick, our
observations so far can be represented as a *partial realization* ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, a function from some
subset of $E$ (i.e., the set of items that we already picked)
to their states (e.g., ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ encodes where we placed sensors and which of them failed).
For notational convenience, we sometimes represent ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ as a relation, so that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq E\times O$ equals$\left\{(e,o):{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}(e)=o\right\}$.
We use the notation $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})=\left\{e:\exists o.(e,o)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right\}$ to refer to the domain of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ (i.e., the set of items observed in ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$).
A partial realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is *consistent* with a realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ if they are equal everywhere in the domain
of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
In this case we write ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
If ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ are both consistent with some ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, and$\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\subseteq\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$, we say ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is a *subrealization* of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$. Equivalently, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is a subrealization
of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ if and only if, when viewed as relations, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$.

Partial realizations are similar to the notion of “belief states” in
Partially Observable Markov Decision Problems (POMDPs), as they encode
the effect of all actions taken (items selected) and observations made, and determine our
posterior belief about the state of the world (i.e., the state of all
items $e$ not yet selected, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)}}:=\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$).

### 2.2 Policies

We encode our adaptive strategy for picking items as a *policy* $\pi$, which is a function from a set of partial realizations to$E$, specifying which item to pick next under a particular
set of observations (e.g., $\pi$ chooses the next sensor location
given where we have placed sensors so far, and whether they failed or
not).
We also allow randomized policies that are functions
from a set of partial realizations to distributions on $E$,
though our emphasis will primarily be on deterministic policies.
If ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is not in the domain of $\pi$, the policy
terminates (stops picking items) upon observation of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
We use $\dom(\pi)$ to denote the domain of $\pi$.
Technically, we require that $\dom(\pi)$ be closed under
subrealizations. That is, if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}\in\dom(\pi)$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is a subrealization of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ then ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\dom(\pi)$.
We use the notation $E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})$ to refer to the set of items selected by $\pi$ under realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$. Each deterministic policy $\pi$ can be associated with a decision
tree $T^{\pi}$ in a natural way (see Fig. [1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 2.2 Policies ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") for
an illustration).
Here, we adopt a policy-centric view that admits concise
notation, though we find the decision tree view to be valuable conceptually.

Since partial realizations are similar to POMDP belief states, our definition of policies is similar to the notion of policies in POMDPs, which are usually defined as functions from belief states to actions. We will further discuss the relationship between the stochastic optimization problems considered in this paper and POMDPs in Section [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S13 "13 Related Work ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

*Figure 1: Illustration of a policy $\pi$, its corresponding
decision tree representation, and the decision tree representation
of $\pi_{[{2}]}$, the level $2$ truncation of $\pi$ (as defined in §[5.1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S5.SS1 "5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")).*

### 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage

We wish to maximize, subject to some constraints, a utility function $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ that depends on which items we pick and which state each item is in (e.g., modeling the total area covered by the working sensors).
Based on this notation, the expected utility of a policy $\pi$ is$f_{\text{avg}}(\pi):=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]$ where the expectation is taken with respect to ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$.
The goal of the *Adaptive Stochastic Maximization* problem is to
find a policy $\pi^{*}$ such that

|  | $\pi^{*}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,max}_{\pi}f_{\text{avg}}(\pi)\text{ subject to }&#124;E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})&#124;\leq k\text{ for all }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},$ |  | (1) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | --- |

where $k$ is a budget on how many items can be picked (e.g., we would like to adaptively choose $k$ sensor locations such that the working sensors provide as much information as possible in expectation).

Alternatively, we can specify a quota $Q$ of utility that we
would like to obtain, and try to find the cheapest policy achieving
that quota (e.g., we would like to achieve a certain amount of information, as cheaply as possible in expectation). Formally, we define the average cost ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}$ of a
policy as the expected number of items it picks, so that${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}:=\mathbb{E}\left[&#124;E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}})&#124;\right]$. Our goal is then to find

|  | $\pi^{*}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\pi}{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\text{ such that }f(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})\geq Q\text{ for all }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},$ |  | (2) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | --- |

i.e., the policy $\pi^{*}$ that minimizes the expected number of
items picked such that under all possible realizations, at least
utility $Q$ is achieved. We call Problem [2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E2 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") the *Adaptive Stochastic Minimum Cost Cover* problem. We will also
consider the problem where we want to minimize the worst-case cost${c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi)}}:=\max_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}{&#124;E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})&#124;}$. This
worst-case cost ${c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi)}}$ is the cost
incurred under adversarially chosen realizations, or equivalently the
depth of the deepest leaf in $T^{\pi}$, the decision tree
associated with $\pi$.

Yet another important variant is to minimize the average time required
by a policy to obtain its utility. Formally, let $u\!\left({{\pi},{t}}\right)$ be
the expected utility obtained by $\pi$ after $t$ steps33 3 For a more formal definition of $u\!\left({{\pi},{t}}\right)$,
see §[15.5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS5 "15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") on page [15.5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS5 "15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").,
let $Q=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]$ be the maximum
possible expected utility,
and define the *min-sum cost* $c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right)$ of a policy as$c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right):=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\left({Q-u\!\left({{\pi},{t}}\right)}\right)$. We then define the *Adaptive
Stochastic Min-Sum Cover* problem as the search for

|  | $\pi^{*}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\pi}c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right).$ |  | (3) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | --- |

Unfortunately, as we will show in §[12](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S12 "12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), even for
linear functions $f$, i.e., those where $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=\sum_{e\in A}w_{e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}$ is simply the sum of weights (depending on the
realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$), Problems ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), ([2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E2 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")),
and ([3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E3 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"))
are hard to approximate
under reasonable complexity theoretic assumptions.
Despite the hardness of the general problems,
in the following sections we will identify conditions that are sufficient to
allow us to approximately solve them.

### 2.4 Incorporating Item Costs

Instead of quantifying the cost of a set $E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})$ by
the number of elements $&#124;E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})&#124;$, we can also
consider the case where each item $e\in E$ has a
cost $c(e)$, and the cost of a set$S\subseteq E$ is $c(S)=\sum_{e\in S}c(e)$. We can
then consider variants of
Problems ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), ([2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E2 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")),
and ([3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E3 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) with the $&#124;E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})&#124;$ replaced by$c(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}))$. For clarity of presentation, we will focus on the unit cost case, i.e., $c(e)=1$ for all $e$,
and explain how our results generalize to the non-uniform case in the Appendix.

## 3 Adaptive Submodularity

We first review the classical notion of submodular set functions, and then introduce the novel notion of adaptive submodularity.

### 3.1 Background on Submodularity

Let us first consider the very special case where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ is
deterministic or, equivalently, $&#124;O&#124;=1$ (e.g., in our sensor
placement applications, sensors never fail). In this case, the
realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ is known to the decision maker in advance, and thus
there is no benefit in adaptive selection.
Given the realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, Problem ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) is equivalent to finding a set $A^{*}\subseteq E$ such that

|  | $A^{*}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,max}_{A\subseteq E}f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})\text{ such that }&#124;A&#124;\leq k.$ |  | (4) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | --- |

For most interesting classes of utility functions $f$, this is an NP-hard optimization problem. However, in many practical problems, such as those mentioned in §[1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S1 "1 Introduction ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), $f(A)=f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ satisfies *submodularity*. A set function $f:2^{E}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$ is called submodular if, whenever $A\subseteq B\subseteq E$ and $e\in E\setminus B$ it holds that

|  | $f(A\cup\{e\})-f(A)\geq f(B\cup\{e\})-f(B),$ |  | (5) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | --- |

i.e., adding $e$ to the smaller set $A$ increases $f$ by at least as
much as adding $e$ to the superset $B$. Furthermore, $f$ is
called *monotone*, if, whenever $A\subseteq B$ it holds that$f(A)\leq f(B)$ (e.g., adding a sensor can never reduce the amount of information obtained). A celebrated result by [Nemhauser et al. (1978)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib54) states
that for monotone submodular functions with $f(\emptyset)=0$, a
simple greedy algorithm that starts with the empty set,$A_{0}=\emptyset$ and chooses

|  | $A_{i+1}=A_{i}\cup\{\mathop{\rm arg\,max}_{e\in E\setminus A_{i}}f(A_{i}\cup\{e\})\}$ |  | (6) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | --- |

guarantees that $f(A_{k})\geq(1-1/e)\max_{&#124;A&#124;\leq k}f(A)$. Thus, the greedy set $A_{k}$ obtains at least a $(1-1/e)$ fraction of the optimal value
achievable using $k$ elements.
Furthermore, [Feige (1998)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib14) shows that this result is tight if$\textrm{P}\neq\textrm{NP}$; under this assumption *no polynomial time algorithm* can do strictly better than the greedy algorithm, i.e., achieve a$(1-1/e+\epsilon)$-approximation for any constant $\epsilon>0$,
even for
the special case of Maximum $k$-Cover where $f(A)$ is the
cardinality of the union of sets indexed by $A$.
Similarly, [Wolsey (1982)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib66) shows that the same greedy algorithm also
near-optimally solves the
deterministic case of Problem ([2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E2 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), called the *Minimum Submodular Cover* problem:

|  | $A^{*}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{A\subseteq E}&#124;A&#124;\text{ such that }f(A)\geq Q.$ |  | (7) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | --- |

Pick the first set $A_{\ell}$ constructed by the greedy
algorithm such that $f(A_{\ell})\geq Q$. Then, for integer-valued
submodular functions, $\ell$ is at most $&#124;A^{*}&#124;(1+\log\max_{e}f(e))$, i.e., the greedy set is at most a logarithmic factor
larger than the smallest set achieving quota $Q$. For the
special case of Set Cover, where $f(A)$ is the cardinality
of a union of sets indexed by $A$, this result matches a lower bound by [Feige (1998)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib14): Unless $\textrm{NP}\subseteq\text{DTIME}(n^{{\mathcal{O}}(\log\log n)})$, Set Cover is hard to approximate by a factor better than $(1-\varepsilon)\ln Q$, where $Q$ is the number of elements to be covered.

Now let us relax the assumption that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ is
deterministic. In this case, we may still want to find a non-adaptive
solution (i.e., a constant policy $\pi_{A}$ that always picks set$A$ independently of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}$) maximizing $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{A})$. If $f$ is *pointwise* submodular, i.e., $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is submodular in$A$ for any fixed ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, the function $f(A)=f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{A})$ is
submodular, since nonnegative linear combinations of submodular
functions remain submodular. Thus, the greedy algorithm allows us to
find a near-optimal *non-adaptive* policy. That is, in our sensor placement example, if we are willing to commit to all locations *before* finding out whether the sensors fail or not, the greedy algorithm can provide a good solution to this non-adaptive problem.

However, in practice, we may be more interested in obtaining a
non-constant policy $\pi$, that *adaptively* chooses items
based on previous observations (e.g., takes into account which sensors
are working before placing the next sensor). In many settings,
selecting items adaptively offers huge advantages, analogous to the
advantage of binary search over sequential (linear)
search44 4 We provide a well–known example in active learning that illustrates this phenomenon
crisply in §[9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"); see Fig. [4](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") on
page [9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). We consider the
general question of the magnitude of the potential benefits of
adaptivity in §[11](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S11 "11 Adaptivity Gap ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") on page [11](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S11 "11 Adaptivity Gap ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") ..
Thus, the question is whether there is a natural extension of submodularity to policies. In the following, we will develop such a notion – *adaptive submodularity*.

### 3.2 Adaptive Monotonicity and Submodularity

The key challenge is to find
appropriate generalizations of monotonicity and
of the diminishing returns condition
([5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3.E5 "In 3.1 Background on Submodularity ‣ 3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")). We begin by again considering the very special
case where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ is deterministic, so that the policies are
non-adaptive. In this case a policy $\pi$ simply specifies a
sequence of items $(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{r})$ which it selects in order.
Monotonicity in this context can be characterized as the property that
“the marginal benefit of selecting an item is always nonnegative,” meaning that for all such sequences $(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{r})$, items $e$ and $1\leq i\leq r$ it holds that $f(\left\{e_{j}:j\leq i\right\}\cup\{e\})-f(\left\{e_{j}:j\leq i\right\})\geq 0$.
Similarly, submodularity can be viewed as the property that
“selecting an item later never increases its marginal benefit,”
meaning that for all sequences $(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{r})$, items$e$, and all $i\leq r$,$f(\left\{e_{j}:j\leq i\right\}\cup\left\{e\right\})-f(\left\{e_{j}:j\leq i\right\})\geq f(\left\{e_{j}:j\leq r\right\}\cup\left\{e\right\})-f(\left\{e_{j}:j\leq r\right\})$.

We take these views of monotonicity and submodularity when defining
their adaptive analogues, by using an appropriate generalization of the marginal benefit.
When moving to the general adaptive setting, the challenge is that
the items’ states are now random and only revealed upon selection. A
natural approach is thus to condition on observations (i.e., partial
realizations of selected items), and take the expectation with respect to the items that we consider selecting.
Hence, we define our adaptive monotonicity and submodularity properties in terms of the *conditional expected marginal benefit* of an item.

###### Definition 1 (Conditional Expected Marginal Benefit)

Given a partial realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and an item $e$, the *conditional expected marginal benefit* of $e$ conditioned on having observed ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, denoted $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$, is

|  | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right):=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{}{}\!f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \bigg&#124;\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$ |  | (8) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | --- |

where the expectation is computed with respect to ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)}}=\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$.
Similarly, the *conditional expected marginal benefit of a
policy* $\pi$ is

|  | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right):=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{}{}\!f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \bigg&#124;\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\mbox{.}$ |  | (9) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | --- |

In our sensor placement example, $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ quantifies the expected amount of additional area covered by placing a
sensor at location $e$, in expectation over the posterior
distribution${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)}\!\left({o}\right)}}:=\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)=o\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$ of whether the sensor will fail or not, and taking into account the
area covered by the placed working sensors as encoded by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
Note that the benefit we have accrued upon observing ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ (and
hence after having selected the items in $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$) is $\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$, which is the
benefit term subtracted out in Eq. ([8](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3.E8 "In Definition 1 (Conditional Expected Marginal Benefit) ‣ 3.2 Adaptive Monotonicity and Submodularity ‣ 3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) and
Eq. ([9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3.E9 "In Definition 1 (Conditional Expected Marginal Benefit) ‣ 3.2 Adaptive Monotonicity and Submodularity ‣ 3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")).
Similarly, the expected total benefit obtained after observing ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and then selecting $e$ is $\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$. The corresponding benefit for
running $\pi$ after observing ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is slightly more complex.
Under realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, the final cumulative benefit will
be $f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$. Taking the
expectation with respect to ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)}}$ and
subtracting out the benefit already obtained by $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ then yields
the conditional expected marginal benefit of $\pi$.

We are now ready to introduce our generalizations of monotonicity and submodularity to the adaptive setting:

###### Definition 2 (Adaptive Monotonicity)

A function $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is *adaptive monotone* with respect to distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ if the conditional expected marginal benefit of any item is
nonnegative, i.e., for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ with $\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]>0$ and all $e\in E$ we have

|  | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\geq 0.$ |  | (10) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

###### Definition 3 (Adaptive Submodularity)

A function $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is *adaptive submodular* with respect to distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ if the conditional expected marginal benefit of any fixed item does not
increase as more items are selected and their states are observed.
Formally, $f$ is adaptive submodular w.r.t. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ if for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ such that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is a subrealization of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ (i.e., ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$), and for all $e\in E\setminus\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$, we have

|  | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\geq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right).$ |  | (11) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

From the decision tree perspective, the condition$\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\geq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)$ amounts to saying that
for any decision tree $T$, if we are at a node $v$ in $T$ which selects an item $e$, and compare the
expected marginal benefit of $e$ selected at $v$ with the expected marginal benefit$e$ would have obtained if it were selected at an ancestor of$v$ in $T$, then the latter must be no smaller than the former.
Note that when comparing the two expected marginal benefits, there is
a difference in both the set of items previously selected
(i.e., $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ vs. $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$) and in the distribution over
realizations (i.e., ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)}}$ vs. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}\right)}}$).
It is also worth emphasizing that adaptive submodularity is defined relative to the distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ over
realizations; it is possible that $f$ is
adaptive submodular with respect to one distribution, but not with
respect to another.

We will give concrete examples of adaptive monotone and adaptive submodular functions that arise in the applications introduced in
§[1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S1 "1 Introduction ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") in §[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), §[7](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S7 "7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), §[8](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8 "8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"),
and §[9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").
In the Appendix, we will explain how the notion of adaptive submodularity can be extended to handle non-uniform costs (since, e.g., the cost of placing a sensor at an easily accessible location may be smaller than at a location that is hard to get to).

### 3.3 Properties of Adaptive Submodular Functions

It can be seen that adaptive monotonicity and adaptive submodularity enjoy similar closure properties as monotone submodular
functions. In particular, if $w_{1},\dots,w_{m}\geq 0$ and$f_{1},\dots,f_{m}$ are adaptive monotone submodular w.r.t. distribution${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$, then $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}f_{i}(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is
adaptive monotone submodular w.r.t. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$.
Similarly, for a fixed constant $c\geq 0$ and adaptive monotone
submodular function $f$, the function $g(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=\min(f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}),c)$ is
adaptive monotone submodular.
Thus, adaptive monotone submodularity is preserved by nonnegative linear combinations and by truncation.
Adaptive monotone submodularity is also preserved by
restriction, so that if$f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is adaptive monotone submodular w.r.t. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$, then for any $e\in E$, the function$g:2^{E\setminus\left\{e\right\}}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ defined by $g(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ for all $A\subseteq E\setminus\left\{e\right\}$ and all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ is also adaptive submodular w.r.t. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$.
Finally, if$f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is adaptive monotone submodular w.r.t. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ then for each partial
realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ the conditional function$g(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=f(A\cup\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is adaptive monotone
submodular w.r.t. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)}}:=\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$.

### 3.4 What Problem Characteristics Suggest Adaptive Submodularity?

Adaptive submodularity is a diminishing returns property for policies.
Speaking informally, it can be applied in situations where there is an objective function
to be optimized does not feature synergies in the benefits of items
conditioned on observations. In some cases, the primary objective
might not have this property, but a suitably chosen proxy of it does, as is
the case with active learning with persistent noise ([Golovin et al., 2010](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib26); [Bellala and Scott, 2010](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib4)).
We give example applications in §[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") through §[9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). It is also worth mentioning
where adaptive submodularity is *not* directly applicable.
An extreme example of synergistic effects between items conditioned on
observations is the class of “treasure hunting” instances used to prove Theorem [26](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem26 "Theorem 26 ‣ 12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") on page [26](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem26 "Theorem 26 ‣ 12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), where the (binary) state of certain
groups of items encode the treasure’s location in a complex manner. Another
problem feature which adaptive submodularity does not directly address is
the possibility that items selection can alter the underlying realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, as
is the case for the problem of optimizing policies for general POMDPs.

## 4 The Adaptive Greedy Policy

The classical non-adaptive greedy algorithm ([6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3.E6 "In 3.1 Background on Submodularity ‣ 3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) has a natural generalization to the adaptive setting.
The *greedy policy* $\pi^{\text{greedy}}$ tries, at each iteration, to
myopically increase the expected objective value, given its current
observations.
That is, suppose$f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is the objective, and${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is the partial realization indicating the states of items selected so
far.
Then the greedy policy will select the item $e$ maximizing the
expected increase in value, conditioned on the observed states of
items it has
already selected (i.e., conditioned on ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$). That is, it
will select $e$ to maximize the conditional expected
marginal benefit $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ as defined in Eq. ([8](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3.E8 "In Definition 1 (Conditional Expected Marginal Benefit) ‣ 3.2 Adaptive Monotonicity and Submodularity ‣ 3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")).
Pseudocode of the adaptive greedy algorithm is given in
Algorithm [1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#algorithm1 "In 4.2 Approximate Greedy Selection ‣ 4 The Adaptive Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). The only difference to the classic,
non-adaptive greedy algorithm studied by [Nemhauser et al. (1978)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib54), is
Line [1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#algorithm1 "In 4.2 Approximate Greedy Selection ‣ 4 The Adaptive Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), where an observation ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e^{*})$ of the
selected item $e^{*}$ is obtained. Note that the algorithms
in this section are presented for Adaptive Stochastic Maximization.
For the coverage objectives, we simply keep selecting items as prescribed by$\pi^{\text{greedy}}$ until achieving the quota on objective value (for the
min-cost objective) or until we have selected every item (for the
min-sum objective).

### 4.1 Incorporating Item Costs

The adaptive greedy algorithm can be naturally modified
to handle non-uniform item costs by replacing its selection rule by

|  | $e^{*}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,max}_{e}\frac{\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)}{c(e)}.$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

In the following, we will focus on the uniform cost case
($c\equiv 1$), and defer the analysis with costs to the Appendix.

### 4.2 Approximate Greedy Selection

In some applications, finding an item maximizing$\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ may be computationally
intractable, and the best we can do is find an $\alpha$-approximation
to the best greedy selection. This means we find an $e^{\prime}$ such that

|  | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e^{\prime}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\max_{e}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right).$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

We call a policy which always
selects such an item an *$\alpha$-approximate greedy policy*.

Input: Budget $k$; ground set $E$; distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$;
function $f$.

Output: Set $A\subseteq E$ of size $k$

begin $A\leftarrow\emptyset$; ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\leftarrow\emptyset$;

for *$i=1$ to $k$* do foreach  *$e\in E\setminus A$* do compute $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[f(A\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})-f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ &#124;\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$ ;

Select $e^{*}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,max}_{e}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$;

Set $A\leftarrow A\cup\{e^{*}\}$;

Observe ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e^{*})$; Set ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\cup\left\{(e^{*},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e^{*}))\right\}$;

end

*Algorithm 1 The adaptive greedy
algorithm, which implements the greedy policy.*

### 4.3 Robustness & Approximate Greedy Selection

As we will show, $\alpha$-approximate greedy policies have
performance guarantees on several problems.
The fact that these performance guarantees of greedy policies are
robust to approximate greedy selection suggests a particular
robustness guarantee against incorrect priors ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$.
Specifically, if our incorrect prior ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p}}^{\prime}$ is such that when we evaluate$\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ we err by a multiplicative factor of at most$\alpha$, then when we compute the greedy policy with respect to${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p}}^{\prime}$ we are actually implementing an $\alpha$-approximate
greedy policy (with respect to the true prior), and hence obtain the
corresponding guarantees. For example, a sufficient condition for
erring by at most a multiplicative factor of $\alpha$ is
that there exists $c\leq 1$ and $d\geq 1$ with $\alpha=d/c$ such that$c\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p}}^{\prime}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)\leq d\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p}}$ is the true prior.

### 4.4 Lazy Evaluations and the Accelerated Adaptive Greedy Algorithm

The definition of adaptive submodularity
allows us to implement an
“accelerated” version of the adaptive greedy algorithm using lazy evaluations
of marginal benefits as originally suggested for the
non-adaptive case by [Minoux (1978)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib51).
The idea is as follows.
Suppose we run $\pi^{\text{greedy}}$ under some fixed realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$,
and select items $e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{k}$.
Let ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i}:=\left\{(e_{j},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e_{j}):j\leq i)\right\}$ be the
partial realizations observed during the run of $\pi^{\text{greedy}}$.
The adaptive greedy algorithm computes $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i}}}\right)$ for all $e\in E$ and$0\leq i<k$, unless $e\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i})$.
Naively, the algorithm thus needs to compute $\Theta(&#124;E&#124;k)$ marginal benefits (which can be expensive to compute).
The key insight is that$i\mapsto\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i}}}\right)$ is nonincreasing for all $e\in E$, because of the adaptive submodularity of the
objective.
Hence, if when deciding which item to select as $e_{i}$ we know$\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e^{\prime}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{j}}}\right)\leq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i}}}\right)$ for some items $e^{\prime}$ and$e$ and $j<i$, then we may conclude $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e^{\prime}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i}}}\right)\leq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i}}}\right)$ and hence
eliminate the need to compute $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e^{\prime}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i}}}\right)$.
The accelerated version of the adaptive greedy algorithm exploits this
observation in a principled manner, by computing $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ for items $e$ in decreasing order of the upper bounds known on
them, until it finds an item whose value is at least as great as the upper bounds of
all other items. Pseudocode of this version of the adaptive greedy algorithm is given in Algorithm [2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#algorithm2 "In 4.4 Lazy Evaluations and the Accelerated Adaptive Greedy Algorithm ‣ 4 The Adaptive Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

In the non-adaptive setting, the use of lazy evaluations has been shown
to significantly reduce running times in practice ([Leskovec et al., 2007](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib45)).
We evaluated the naive and accelerated implementations of the
adaptive greedy algorithm on two sensor selection problems, and obtained
speedup factors that range from roughly $4$ to $40$ for those problems.
See §[10](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10 "10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") on page [10](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10 "10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") for details.

Input: Budget $k$; ground set $E$; distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$;
function $f$.

Output: Set $A\subseteq E$ of size $k$

begin $A\leftarrow\emptyset$; ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\leftarrow\emptyset$;
Priority Queue $Q\leftarrow\textsc{empty\_queue}$;

foreach  *$e\in E$* do $Q.\operatorname{insert}(e,+\infty)$;

for *$i=1$ to $k$* do $\delta_{\max}\leftarrow-\infty$; $e_{\max}\leftarrow\textsc{null}$;

while  *$\delta_{\max}<Q.\operatorname{maxPriority}(\,)$* do $e\leftarrow Q.\operatorname{pop}(\,)$;

$\delta\leftarrow\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[f(A\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})-f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ &#124;\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$;

$Q.\operatorname{insert}(e,\delta)$;

if  *$\delta_{\max}<\delta$* then $\delta_{\max}\leftarrow\delta$; $e_{\max}\leftarrow e$;

$A\leftarrow A\cup\left\{e_{\max}\right\}$; $Q.\operatorname{remove}(e_{\max})$;

Observe ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e_{\max})$; Set ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\cup\left\{(e_{\max},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e_{\max}))\right\}$;

end

*Algorithm 2 The accelerated version of the adaptive greedy
algorithm. Here, $Q.\operatorname{insert}(e,\delta)$ inserts $e$ with
priority $\delta$, $Q.\operatorname{pop}(\,)$ removes and returns the
item with greatest priority, $Q.\operatorname{maxPriority}(\,)$ returns the
maximum priority of the elements in $Q$, and $Q.\operatorname{remove}(e)$ deletes $e$ from $Q$.*

## 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy

In this section we show that if the objective function is adaptive submodular with respect to the probabilistic model of the
environment in which we operate, then the greedy policy inherits
precisely the performance guarantees of the greedy
algorithm for classic (non-adaptive) submodular maximization and submodular coverage
problems, such as Maximum $k$-Cover and Minimum
Set Cover, as well as min-sum submodular
coverage problems, such as Min-Sum Set Cover.
In fact, we will show that this holds true more generally:$\alpha$–approximate greedy policies inherit precisely the performance guarantees of$\alpha$–approximate greedy algorithms for these classic problems. These guarantees suggest that adaptive submodularity is an
appropriate generalization of submodularity to policies. In this section
we focus on the unit cost case (i.e., every item has the same cost). In the Appendix we
provide the proofs omitted in this section, and
show how our results extend to non-uniform item costs if we greedily
maximize the expected benefit/cost ratio.

### 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective

In this section we consider the maximum coverage objective, where the
goal is to select $k$ items adaptively to maximize their expected
value. The task of maximizing expected value subject to more complex
constraints, such as matroid constraints and intersections of matroid
constraints, is considered in the work of [Golovin and Krause (2011b)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib24).
Before stating our result, we require the following definition.

###### Definition 4 (Policy Truncation)

For a policy $\pi$, define the *level-$k$-truncation* $\pi_{[{k}]}$ of $\pi$ to be the policy obtained by
running $\pi$ until it terminates or until it selects $k$ items,
and then terminating. Formally,$\dom(\pi_{[{k}]})=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\dom(\pi)\ :\ &#124;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}&#124;<k\right\}$, and $\pi_{[{k}]}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})=\pi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\dom(\pi_{[{k}]})$.

We have the following result, which generalizes the
classic result of the work of [Nemhauser et al. (1978)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib54) that the greedy algorithm
achieves a $(1-1/e)$-approximation to the problem of
maximizing monotone submodular functions under a
cardinality constraint. By setting $\ell=k$ and $\alpha=1$ in
Theorem [5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 5 ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), we see
that the greedy policy which selects $k$ items adaptively obtains at
least $(1-1/e)$ of the value of the optimal policy that selects $k$ items adaptively,
measured with respect to $f_{\text{avg}}$.
For a proof see
Theorem [38](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem38 "Theorem 38 ‣ 15.3 The Max-Cover Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") in
Appendix [15.3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS3 "15.3 The Max-Cover Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"),
which generalizes Theorem [5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 5 ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") to nonuniform item costs.

###### Theorem 5

Fix any $\alpha\geq 1$.
If $f$ is adaptive monotone and adaptive submodular with respect to the
distribution${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$, and $\pi$ is an$\alpha$-approximate greedy policy, then for all policies $\pi^{*}$ and positive
integers $\ell$ and $k$,

|  | $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}]})>\left({1-e^{-\ell/\alpha k}}\right)f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{*}_{[{k}]})\mbox{.}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

In particular, with $\ell=k$ this implies any $\alpha$-approximate
greedy policy achieves a $\left({1-e^{-1/\alpha}}\right)$ approximation to
the expected reward of the best
policy, if both are terminated after running for an equal number of steps.

If the greedy rule can be implemented only with small *absolute* error rather than small *relative* error, i.e., $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e^{\prime}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\geq\max_{e}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)-\varepsilon$, an argument similar to that used to
prove Theorem [5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 5 ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") shows that

|  | $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}]})\geq\left({1-e^{-\ell/k}}\right)f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{*}_{[{k}]})-\ell\varepsilon\mbox{.}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

This is important, since small absolute error can always be achieved
(with high probability) whenever $f$ can be evaluated efficiently, and
sampling ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)}}$ is efficient. In this case, we can approximate

|  | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\approx\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{i})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{i})\bigr]\mbox{,}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{i}$ are sampled i.i.d. from ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)}}$.

#### 5.1.1 Data Dependent Bounds

For the maximum coverage objective, adaptive submodular functions have
another attractive feature: they allow us to obtain data dependent
bounds on the optimum, in a manner similar to the bounds for the non-adaptive case ([Minoux, 1978](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib51)).
Consider the non-adaptive problem of maximizing a monotone submodular
function $f:2^{A}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ subject to the
constraint $&#124;A&#124;\leq k$. Let $A^{*}$ be an optimal solution,
and fix any $A\subseteq E$. Then

|  | $f(A^{*})\leq f(A)+\max_{B:&#124;B&#124;\leq k}\sum_{e\in B}\left({f(A\cup\left\{e\right\})-f(A)}\right)$ |  | (12) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

because setting $B=A^{*}$ we have$f(A^{*})\leq f(A\cup B)\leq f(A)+\sum_{e\in B}\left({f(A\cup\left\{e\right\})-f(A)}\right)$.
Note that unlike the original objective, we can easily compute$\max_{B:&#124;B&#124;\leq k}\sum_{e\in B}\left({f(A\cup\left\{e\right\})-f(A)}\right)$ by computing $\delta(e):=f(A\cup\left\{e\right\})-f(A)$ for each$e$, and summing the $k$ largest values. Hence we can quickly compute
an upper bound on our distance from the optimal value, $f(A^{*})-f(A)$. In practice, such data-dependent bounds can be much tighter than the problem-independent performance guarantees of [Nemhauser et al. (1978)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib54) for the greedy algorithm ([Leskovec et al., 2007](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib45)). Further note that these bounds hold for *any* set $A$, not just sets selected by the greedy algorithm.

These data dependent bounds have the following analogue for adaptive monotone
submodular functions. See
Appendix [15.2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS2 "15.2 Adaptive Data Dependent Bounds with Costs ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") for a proof.

###### Lemma 6 (The Adaptive Data Dependent Bound)

Suppose we have made observations ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ after
selecting $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$. Let $\pi^{*}$ be any policy such that$&#124;E({\pi^{*}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})&#124;\leq k$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
Then for adaptive monotone submodular $f$

|  | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi^{*}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\ \leq\ \max_{A\subseteq E,&#124;A&#124;\leq k}\ \sum_{e\in A}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right).$ |  | (13) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Thus, after running *any* policy $\pi$, we can efficiently compute a bound on the additional benefit that the optimal solution $\pi^{*}$ could obtain beyond the reward of $\pi$. We do that by computing the conditional expected marginal benefits for all elements $e$, and summing the $k$ largest of them. Note that these bounds can be computed on the fly when running the greedy algorithm, in a similar manner as discussed by [Leskovec et al. (2007)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib45) for the non-adaptive setting.

### 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective

Another natural objective is to minimize the number of items selected
while ensuring that a sufficient level of value is obtained. This
leads to the *Adaptive Stochastic Minimum Cost Coverage* problem
described in §[2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2 "2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), namely$\pi^{*}\in\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\pi}{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\text{ such that }f(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})\geq Q\text{ for all }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$. Recall that${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}$ is the expected cost of $\pi$, which in the unit
cost case equals the expected number of items
selected by $\pi$, i.e., ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}:=\mathbb{E}\left[&#124;E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}})&#124;\right]$.
If the objective is adaptive monotone submodular,
this is an adaptive version of the Minimum Submodular Cover problem
(described on line ([7](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3.E7 "In 3.1 Background on Submodularity ‣ 3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) in
§[3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3.SS1 "3.1 Background on Submodularity ‣ 3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")). Recall that the greedy algorithm is known to give a$(\ln(Q)+1)$-approximation for Minimum Submodular Cover
assuming the coverage function is integer-valued in addition to being monotone submodular ([Wolsey, 1982](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib66)).
Adaptive Stochastic Minimum Cost Coverage is also related
to the *(Noisy) Interactive Submodular Set Cover* problem studied by [Guillory and Bilmes (2010)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib33); [Guillory and Bilmes (2011)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib34),
which considers the worst-case setting
(i.e., there is no distribution over states; instead states are
realized in an adversarial manner).
Similar results for active learning have been proved
by [Kosaraju et al. (1999)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib40) and [Dasgupta (2004)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib10), as we discuss in more detail in §[9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

We assume throughout this section that there exists a quality
threshold $Q$ such that $f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, and
for all $S\subseteq E$ and all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, $f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})\leq Q$. Note that, as discussed in Section [3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3 "3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), if we replace $f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ by a new function $g(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=\min(f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}),Q^{\prime})$ for some constant $Q^{\prime}$, $g$ will be adaptive submodular if $f$ is. Thus, if $f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ varies across realizations, we can instead use the greedy algorithm on the function truncated at some threshold $Q^{\prime}\leq\min_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ achievable by all realizations.

In contrast to Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, for the coverage
problem additional subtleties arise.
In particular, it is not enough
that a policy $\pi$ achieves value $Q$ for the true realization;
in order for $\pi$ to terminate, it also requires a proof of this
fact. Formally, we require that $\pi$ *covers* $f$:

###### Definition 7 (Coverage)

Let ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}(\pi,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ be the partial realization encoding all
states observed during the execution of $\pi$ under realization${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
Given $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}$, we say a policy$\pi$ *covers* ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ *with respect to* $f$ if$f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})=f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
We say that $\pi$ *covers* $f$ if it covers every realization
with respect to $f$.

Coverage is defined in such a way that upon terminating,$\pi$ might not know which realization
is the true one, but has
guaranteed that it has achieved the maximum reward in every possible
case (i.e., for every realization consistent with its observations). We obtain results for both the average and worst-case cost objectives.
