#### 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost

Before presenting our approximation guarantee for the Adaptive Stochastic Minimum Cost Coverage,
we introduce a special class of instances, called *self–certifying* instances.
We make this distinction because
the greedy policy has stronger performance guarantees for
self–certifying instances, and such instances arise naturally in
applications.
For example, the Stochastic Submodular Cover and Stochastic Set Cover
instances in §[7](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S7 "7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), the Adaptive Viral Marketing instances in
§[8](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8 "8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), and the Pool-Based Active Learning instances
in §[9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") are all self–certifying.

###### Definition 8 (Self–Certifying Instances)

An instance of Adaptive Stochastic Minimum Cost Coverage is *self–certifying* if whenever a policy achieves the maximum possible
value for the true realization it immediately has a proof of this
fact.
Formally, an instance $(f,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}})$ is self–certifying if
for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}$, and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ such that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, we have$f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ if and only if$f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})=f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$.

One class of self–certifying instances which commonly arise are those in which$f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ depends only on the state of items in $A$, and in which
there is a uniform maximum amount of reward that can be obtained
across realizations.
Formally, we have the following observation.

###### Proposition 9

Fix an instance $(f,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}})$.
If there exists $Q$ such that $f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q$ for
all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ and there exists some $g:2^{E\times O}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ such that $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=g\left({\left\{(e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)):e\in A\right\}}\right)$ for all $A$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$,
then $(f,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}})$ is self–certifying.

Proof  Fix ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}$, and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ such that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$. Assuming the existence of $g$ and treating ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ as a relation, we have$f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=g({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})=f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$.
Hence $f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q=f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ if and only if$f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})=Q=f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$.

For our results on minimum cost coverage, we also need a stronger monotonicity condition and a stronger submodularity condition:

###### Definition 10 (Strong Adaptive Monotonicity)

A function $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}$ is *strongly adaptive monotone* with respect to${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ if, informally “selecting more items never hurts” with respect to the expected reward.
Formally, for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, all $e\notin\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$, and all
possible outcomes $o\in O$ such that$\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)=o\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]>0$,
we require

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)=o\right]\mbox{.}$ |  | (14) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Strong adaptive monotonicity implies
adaptive monotonicity, as the latter means that “selecting more items
never hurts in expectation,” i.e.,

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right].$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

To define strong adaptive submodularity, we first need the following extension
of $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$:

###### Definition 11 (Conditional Expected Marginal Benefit (Extended version))

Given partial realizations
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$, let

|  | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right):=\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \bigg&#124;\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}\right]$ |  | (15) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

###### Definition 12 (Strong Adaptive Submodularity)

A function $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is *strongly adaptive submodular* with respect to distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ if it is adaptive submodular and moreover the expected marginal benefit of any
fixed item does not increase as more items are selected and their states are
observed, conditioned on the (item, observation) pairs. Formally, $f$ is
adaptive submodular w.r.t. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ if for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ such that${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is a subrealization of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ (i.e., ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$), and
for all $e\in E\setminus\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$, we have

|  | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)\geq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right).$ |  | (16) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

In other words, conditioning on ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$, adding items$\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})\setminus\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ cannot increase the expected marginal benefit of $e$.

A sufficient condition for strong adaptive submodularity with respect to${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ is that the function be adaptive submodular and *pointwise submodular* (i.e., $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is submodular in $A$ for any fixed ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$), as we prove in
Appendix [15.4](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS4 "15.4 The Min-Cost-Cover Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). It is worth noting that pointwise
submodularity is not sufficient to establish adaptive submodularity.
A simple counterexample is$f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=&#124;\left\{e:e\in S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)=1\right\}&#124;$, with${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}=1/2$ if $\forall e\in E,\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)=1$ and${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}=1/2$ if $\forall e\in E,\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)=0$.
In that case, $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{\emptyset}}\right)=1/2$ yet $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{\left\{(e^{\prime},1)\right\}}}\right)=1$ for any $e^{\prime}\neq e$.

We now state our main result for the average case cost ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}$:

###### Theorem 13

Suppose $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is strongly adaptive submodular and strongly adaptive
monotone with respect to ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ and there exists $Q$ such that$f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
Let $\eta$ be any value such that$f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})>Q-\eta$ implies $f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q$ for all $S$ and${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
Let $\delta=\min_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ be the minimum probability of
any realization.
Let $\pi_{\text{avg}}^{*}$ be an optimal policy
minimizing the expected number of items selected
to guarantee every realization is covered.
Let $\pi$ be an $\alpha$-approximate
greedy policy with respect to the item costs.
Then in general

|  | ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\leq\alpha\,{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi_{\text{avg}}^{*})}}\left({\ln\left({\frac{Q}{\delta\eta}}\right)+1}\right)^{2}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

and for self–certifying instances

|  | ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\leq\alpha\,{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi_{\text{avg}}^{*})}}\left({\ln\left({\frac{Q}{\eta}}\right)+1}\right)^{2}\mbox{.}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

Note that if $\operatorname{range}(f)\subset\mathbb{Z}$, then $\eta=1$ is a valid
choice, so for general and
self–certifying instances we have ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\leq\alpha\,{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi_{\text{avg}}^{*})}}\left({\ln(Q/\delta)+1}\right)^{2}$ and ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\leq\alpha\,{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi_{\text{avg}}^{*})}}\left({\ln(Q)+1}\right)^{2}$, respectively.

##### Historical Note:

An earlier version of Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") claimed
logarithmic approximation factors
rather than the squared–logarithmic factors
present here. Unfortunately, the proof was flawed as pointed out by [Nan and Saligrama (2017)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib53).
Determining whether the logarithmic bounds hold remains an interesting open
problem.
In particular, it remains open whether${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\leq\alpha\,{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi_{\text{avg}}^{*})}}\left({\ln\left({\frac{Q}{\delta\eta}}\right)+1}\right)$ for general instances and ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\leq\alpha\,{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi_{\text{avg}}^{*})}}\left({\ln\left({\frac{Q}{\eta}}\right)+1}\right)$ for self–certifying instances under the conditions specified by Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). It also remains open whether the strong adaptive submodularity condition is required.

#### 5.2.2 Minimizing the Worst-Case Cost

For the worst-case cost ${c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi)}}:=\max_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}{&#124;E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})&#124;}$, strong adaptive monotonicity and strong submodularity are not required;
adaptive monotonicity and adaptive submodularity suffice. We obtain the following result.

###### Theorem 14

Suppose $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is adaptive monotone and adaptive submodular
with respect to ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$, and
let $\eta$ be any value such that$f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})>f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})-\eta$ implies $f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ for all $S$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
Let $\delta=\min_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ be the minimum probability of
any realization.
Let $\pi_{wc}^{*}$ be the optimal policy
minimizing the worst-case number of queries
to guarantee every realization is covered. Let $\pi$ be an $\alpha$-approximate
greedy policy.
Finally, let $Q:=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})\right]$ be the
maximum possible expected reward.
Then

|  | ${c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi)}}\leq\alpha\,{c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi_{wc}^{*})}}\,\left({\ln\left({\frac{Q}{\delta\eta}}\right)+1}\right)\mbox{.}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

The proofs of
Theorems [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") and [14](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem14 "Theorem 14 ‣ 5.2.2 Minimizing the Worst-Case Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") are given in
Appendix [15.4](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS4 "15.4 The Min-Cost-Cover Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

Thus, even though adaptive submodularity is defined w.r.t. a particular
distribution, perhaps surprisingly, the adaptive greedy algorithm is
competitive even in the case of adversarially chosen realizations,
against a policy optimized to minimize the worst-case
cost. Theorem [14](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem14 "Theorem 14 ‣ 5.2.2 Minimizing the Worst-Case Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") therefore suggests
that if we do not have a strong prior, we can obtain the strongest
guarantees if we choose a distribution that is “as uniform as
possible” (i.e., maximizes $\delta$) while still guaranteeing
adaptive submodularity.

#### 5.2.3 Discussion

Note that the approximation factor for self–certifying instances
in Theorem [14](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem14 "Theorem 14 ‣ 5.2.2 Minimizing the Worst-Case Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") reduces to the $(\ln(Q)+1)$-approximation
guarantee for the greedy algorithm for Set Cover instances with $Q$ elements, in the case of a deterministic distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$.
Moreover, with a deterministic distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ there is no distinction between average-case and
worst-case cost.
Hence, an immediate corollary of the result of [Feige (1998)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib14) mentioned in §[3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S3 "3 Adaptive Submodularity ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") is that for every constant $\epsilon>0$ there is
no polynomial time$(1-\epsilon)\ln\left({Q/\eta}\right)$ approximation algorithm for
self–certifying instances of
Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover, under either the ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\cdot)}}$ or
the ${c_{\text{wc}}{(\cdot)}}$ objective,
unless $\textrm{NP}\subseteq\text{DTIME}(n^{{\mathcal{O}}(\log\log n)})$.
It remains open to determine whether or not
Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover with the worst-case cost objective
admits a $\ln\left({Q/\eta}\right)+1$ approximation for self–certifying instances
via a polynomial time algorithm,
and in particular whether the greedy policy has such an approximation guarantee.
However, in Lemma [50](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem50 "Lemma 50 ‣ Putting it all together ‣ Proof of Theorem ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") we show that Feige’s result also implies
there is no $(1-\epsilon)\ln\left({Q/\delta\eta}\right)$ polynomial time approximation
algorithm for general (non self-certifying) instances of
Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover under either objective, unless $\textrm{NP}\subseteq\text{DTIME}(n^{{\mathcal{O}}(\log\log n)})$. In that sense,
Theorem [14](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem14 "Theorem 14 ‣ 5.2.2 Minimizing the Worst-Case Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") is best-possible
and Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") cannot be improved by more than
a logarithmic factor and under
reasonable complexity-theoretic assumptions.

### 5.3 The Min-Sum Cover Objective

Yet another natural objective is the *min-sum* objective, in
which an unrealized reward of $x$ incurs a cost of $x$ in each time
step, and the goal is to minimize the total cost incurred.

#### 5.3.1 Background on the Non-adaptive Min-Sum Cover Problem

In the non-adaptive setting, perhaps the simplest form of a coverage
problem with this objective is the *Min-Sum Set Cover* problem ([Feige et al., 2004](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib16)) in which the input is a set system $(U,{\mathcal{S}})$, the output
is a permutation of the sets $\langle S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{m}\rangle$, and the
goal is to minimize the sum of element *coverage times*, where the
coverage time of $u$ is the index of the first set that contains
it (e.g., it is $j$ if $u\in S_{j}$ and $u\notin S_{i}$ for all $i<j$). In this problem and its generalizations
the min-sum objective is useful in modeling processing costs in
certain applications, for example in ordering diagnostic tests to
identify a disease cheaply ([Kaplan et al., 2005](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib38)), in ordering multiple filters to be applied to
database records while processing a query ([Munagala et al., 2005](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib52)), or in ordering multiple
heuristics to run on boolean satisfiability instances as a means to
solve them faster in practice ([Streeter and Golovin, 2008](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib65)).
A particularly expressive generalization of min-sum set cover has been
studied under the names *Min-Sum Submodular Cover* ([Streeter and Golovin, 2008](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib65)) and$L_{1}$-*Submodular Set Cover* ([Golovin et al., 2008](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib25)).
The former paper extends the greedy algorithm to a natural online variant of the
problem, while the latter studies a parameterized family of$L_{p}$-*Submodular Set Cover* problems in which the objective is
analogous to minimizing the $L_{p}$ norm of the coverage times for
Min-Sum Set Cover instances.
In the Min-Sum Submodular Cover problem, there is a monotone submodular function $f:2^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ defining the reward obtained from a collection
of elements55 5 To encode Min-Sum Set Cover instance$(U,{\mathcal{S}})$, let $E:={\mathcal{S}}$ and $f(A):=&#124;\cup_{e\in A}e&#124;$, where each $e\in E$ is a subset of
elements in $U$..
There is an integral cost $c(e)$ for each element, and the output is a
sequence of all of the elements $\sigma=\langle e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle$.
For each $t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$, we define the set of elements in
the sequence $\sigma$ within a budget of $t$:

|  | $\sigma_{[{t}]}:=\left\{e_{i}:\sum_{j\leq i}c(e_{j})\leq t\right\}.$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

The cost we wish to minimize is then

|  | $c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\sigma}\right):=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\left({f(E)-f(\sigma_{[{t}]})}\right).$ |  | (17) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

[Feige et al. (2004)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib16) proved that for Min-Sum Set cover, the greedy
algorithm achieves a $4$-approximation to the minimum cost, and also
that this is optimal in the sense that no polynomial time algorithm can achieve a$(4-\epsilon)$-approximation, for any $\epsilon>0$, unless $\textrm{P}=\textrm{NP}$.
Interestingly, the greedy algorithm also achieves a $4$-approximation
for the more general Min-Sum Submodular Cover problem as
well ([Streeter and Golovin, 2008](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib65); [Golovin et al., 2008](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib25)).

#### 5.3.2 The Adaptive Stochastic Min-Sum Cover Problem

In this
article, we extend the result of [Streeter and Golovin (2008)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib65) and [Golovin et al. (2008)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib25) to an
adaptive version of Min-Sum Submodular Cover.
For clarity’s sake we will consider the unit-cost case here (i.e., $c(e)=1$ for all$e$); we show how to extend adaptive submodularity to handle general
costs in the Appendix.
In the adaptive version of the problem, $\pi_{[{t}]}$ plays the role of$\sigma_{[{t}]}$, and $f_{\text{avg}}$ plays the role of $f$. The goal is to
find a policy $\pi$ minimizing

|  | $c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right):=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{t}]})}\right)=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\left({f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})-f(E(\pi_{[{t}]},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})}\right).$ |  | (18) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

We call this problem the *Adaptive Stochastic Min-Sum Cover* problem.
The key difference between this objective and the minimum cost
cover objective is that here, the cost at each step is only the fractional
extent that we have not covered the true realization, whereas in the
minimum cost cover objective we are charged in full in each step until
we have completely covered the true realization (according to
Definition [7](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem7 "Definition 7 (Coverage) ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")).
We prove the following result for the Adaptive Stochastic Min-Sum Cover problem with arbitrary item costs in Appendix [15.5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS5 "15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

###### Theorem 15

Fix any $\alpha\geq 1$.
If $f$ is adaptive monotone and adaptive submodular with respect to the
distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$, $\pi$ is an$\alpha$-approximate greedy policy with respect to the item costs, and $\pi^{*}$ is any policy,
then $c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right)\leq 4\alpha\,c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi^{*}}\right)$.

## 6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization

As our first application, consider the sensor placement problem
introduced in §[1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S1 "1 Introduction ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). Suppose we would like to monitor a
spatial phenomenon such as temperature in a building. We discretize
the environment into a set $E$ of locations. We would like
to pick a subset $A\subseteq E$ of $k$ locations that is most
“informative”, where we use a set function $\hat{f}(A)$ to quantify the informativeness of placement $A$. [Krause and Guestrin (2007)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib42) show that many natural objective functions (such as reduction in predictive uncertainty measured in terms of Shannon entropy with conditionally independent observations) are monotone submodular.

Now consider the problem,
where the informativeness of a sensor is unknown before deployment (e.g., when deploying cameras for surveillance, the location of objects and their associated occlusions may not be known in advance, or varying amounts of noise may reduce the sensing range). We can model this extension
by assigning a state ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)\in O$ to each
possible location, indicating the extent to which a sensor placed at location$e$ is working. To quantify the value of a set of sensor deployments
under a realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ indicating to what extent the various
sensors are working, we first define $(e,{o})$ for each $e\in E$ and $o\in O$, which represents the placement of a sensor
at location $e$ which is in state$o$. We then suppose there is a function$\hat{f}:2^{E\times O}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ which
quantifies the informativeness of a set of sensor deployments in arbitrary
states. (Note $\hat{f}$ is a set function taking a set of (sensor
deployment, state) pairs as input.)
The utility $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ of placing sensors at the locations in $A$ under realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ is then

|  | $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=\hat{f}(\left\{(e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)):e\in A\right\}).$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

We aim to adaptively place $k$ sensors to maximize our expected utility.
We assume that sensor failures at each location are independent of each other, i.e.,$\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\right]=\prod_{e\in E}\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)\right],$ where $\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)=o\right]$ is the probability that a sensor
placed at location $e$ will be in state $o$. [Asadpour et al. (2008)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib3) studied
a special case of our problem,
in which sensors either fail completely (in which case they
contribute no value at all) or work perfectly,
under the name *Stochastic Submodular Maximization*. They proved that the adaptive greedy
algorithm obtains a$(1-1/e)$ approximation to the
optimal adaptive policy, provided $\hat{f}$ is monotone submodular.
We extend their result to multiple types of failures
by showing that $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is adaptive submodular with respect to distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ and then invoking Theorem [5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 5 ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). Fig. [2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S7.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 7.1 A Special Case: The Stochastic Set Coverage Problem ‣ 7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") illustrates an instance of Stochastic Submodular Maximization where $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is the cardinality of union of sets index by $A$ and parameterized by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.

###### Theorem 16

Fix a prior such that$\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\right]=\prod_{e\in E}\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)\right]$ and an integer $k$,
and let the objective function$\hat{f}:2^{E\times O}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ be monotone submodular.
Let $\pi$ be any $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy attempting to maximize $f$,
and let $\pi^{*}$ be any policy. Then
for all positive
integers $\ell$,

|  | $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}]})\geq\left({1-e^{-\ell/\alpha k}}\right)f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{*}_{[{k}]})\mbox{.}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

In particular, if $\pi$ is the greedy policy (i.e., $\alpha=1$) and $\ell=k$, then$f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{k}]})\geq\left({1-\frac{1}{e}}\right)f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}).$

Proof  We prove Theorem [16](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem16 "Theorem 16 ‣ 6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") by first proving $f$ is adaptive monotone
and adaptive submodular in
this model, and then applying Theorem [5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 5 ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").
Adaptive monotonicity is readily proved after observing that$f(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is monotone for each ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
Moving on to adaptive submodularity, fix any ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ such that${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ and any $e\notin\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$.
We aim to show $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)\leq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$.
Intuitively, this is clear, as $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)$ is the expected marginal
benefit of adding $e$ to a larger base set than
is the case with $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$, namely $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$ as
compared to $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$, and the realizations are independent.
To prove it rigorously,
we define a coupled distribution $\mu$ over pairs of realizations ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ such that${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e^{\prime})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}(e^{\prime})$ for all $e^{\prime}\notin\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$.
Formally,$\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})=\prod_{e\in E\setminus\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})}\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)\right]$ if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$, and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e^{\prime})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}(e^{\prime})$ for all $e^{\prime}\notin\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$;
otherwise $\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})=0$.
(Note that $\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})>0$ implies ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e^{\prime})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}(e^{\prime})$ for all $e^{\prime}\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ as well,
since ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$, and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$.)
Also note that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)}}=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}}\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}\right)}}=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$.
Calculating$\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)$ and $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ using $\mu$, we see
that for any $({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$ in the support of $\mu$,

|  | $\displaystyle f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$ | $\displaystyle=$ | $\displaystyle\hat{f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}\cup\left\{(e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}(e))\right\})-\hat{f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}))$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | ------------------------------ | ------------------------------ | --- |
|  |                                | $\displaystyle\leq$ | $\displaystyle\hat{f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\cup\left\{(e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e))\right\})-\hat{f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))$ |  |
|  |                                | $\displaystyle=$ | $\displaystyle f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ |  |

from the submodularity of $\hat{f}$.
Hence

|  | $\begin{array}[]{lllll}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)&=&\sum_{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})}\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})\left({f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})}\right)&&\\[8.53581pt] &\leq&\sum_{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})}\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})\left({f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})}\right)&=&\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\end{array}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

which completes the proof.

## 7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage

Suppose that instead of wishing to adaptively place $k$ unreliable sensors
to maximize
the utility of the information obtained, as discussed
in §[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"),
we have a quota on utility and wish to adaptively place the minimum number of
unreliable sensors to achieve this quota. This amounts to
a minimum-cost coverage version of the
Stochastic Submodular Maximization problem introduced
in §[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), which we call *Stochastic Submodular Coverage*.

As in §[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), in the Stochastic Submodular Coverage problem we suppose there is a function$\hat{f}:2^{E\times O}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ which quantifies
the utility of a set of sensors in arbitrary states.
Also, the states of each sensor are independent, so that$\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\right]=\prod_{e\in E}\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)\right]$.
The goal is to obtain a quota $Q$ of utility at minimum cost.
Thus, we define our objective as$f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=\min\left\{Q,\hat{f}(\left\{(e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)):e\in A\right\})\right\}$, and want to
find a policy $\pi$ covering every realization
and minimizing${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}:=\mathbb{E}\left[&#124;E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}})&#124;\right]$.
We additionally assume that this quota can always be obtained using
sufficiently many sensor placements; formally, this amounts to $f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
We obtain the following result, whose proof we defer until the end of this section.

###### Theorem 17

Fix a prior with independent sensor states s.t. $\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\right]=\prod_{e\in E}\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)\right]$,
and let $\hat{f}:2^{E\times O}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ be a mon. submodular function.
Fix $Q\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ s.t.$f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=\min\left({Q,\ \hat{f}(\left\{(e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)):e\in A\right\})}\right)$ satisfies $f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
Let $\eta$ be any value such that$f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})>Q-\eta$ implies $f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q$ for all $S$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
Finally, let $\pi$ be an $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy for maximizing$f$, and let $\pi^{*}$ be any policy. Then

|  | ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\leq\alpha\,{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi^{*})}}\left({\ln\left({\frac{Q}{\eta}}\right)+1}\right)^{2}\mbox{.}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

### 7.1 A Special Case: The Stochastic Set Coverage Problem

The Stochastic Submodular Coverage problem is a generalization of the *Stochastic Set Coverage* problem ([Goemans and Vondrák, 2006](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib21)).
In Stochastic Set Coverage the underlying
submodular objective $\hat{f}$ is the number of elements covered in
some input set system. In other words, there is a ground set $U$ of$n$ elements to be covered, and items $E$ such that each item$e$ is associated with a distribution over subsets of $U$. When
an item is selected, a set is sampled from its distribution, as
illustrated in Fig. [2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S7.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 7.1 A Special Case: The Stochastic Set Coverage Problem ‣ 7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). The
problem is to adaptively select items until all elements of $U$ are covered by sampled sets,
while minimizing the expected number of items selected.
Like us, [Goemans and Vondrák](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib21) also assume that
the subsets are sampled independently for each item,
and every element of $U$ can
be covered in every realization, so that $f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=&#124;U&#124;$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.

*Figure 2: Illustration of part of a Stochastic Set Cover instance. Shown are
the supports of two distributions over sets, indexed by items $e$ (marked in blue) and $e^{\prime}$ (yellow).*

[Goemans and Vondrák](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib21) primarily
investigated the adaptivity gap (quantifying how much adaptive policies can outperform non-adaptive policies) of Stochastic Set Coverage, for variants in which
items can be repeatedly selected or not, and prove adaptivity gaps of$\Theta(\log n)$ in the former case, and between $\Omega(n)$ and${\mathcal{O}}(n^{2})$ in the latter. They also provide an $n$-approximation
algorithm.
More recently, [Liu et al. (2008)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib47) considered a special case of
Stochastic Set Coverage in which each item may be in one of two
states.
They were motivated by a streaming database problem, in which a
collection of queries sharing common filters must all be evaluated on
a stream element. They transform the problem to a Stochastic Set
Coverage instance in which (filter, query) pairs are to be covered by filter
evaluations; which pairs are covered by a filter depends on the
(binary) outcome of evaluating it on the stream element.
The resulting instances satisfy the assumption that
every element of $U$ can be covered in every realization.
They study, among other algorithms, the adaptive greedy algorithm
specialized to this setting, and show that if
the subsets are sampled independently for each item, so that$\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\right]=\prod_{e}\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)\right]$,
then it is an $\mathcal{H}_{n}:=\sum_{x=1}^{n}\frac{1}{x}$ approximation.
(Recall $\ln(n)\leq\mathcal{H}_{n}\leq\ln(n)+1$ for all $n\geq 1$.)
Moreover, [Liu et al.](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib47) report that it
empirically outperforms a number of other algorithms in their experiments.

The adaptive submodularity framework allows us to prove approximate results
for richer item distributions over subsets of $U$ than considered by [Liu et al. (2008)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib47) as a corollary of Theorem [17](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem17 "Theorem 17 ‣ 7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").
Specifically, we obtain a $(\ln(n)+1)^{2}$-approximation for the Stochastic Set Coverage problem with arbitrarily many outcomes for each stochastic set,
where $n:=&#124;U&#124;$.

We model the Stochastic Set Coverage problem by letting ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)\subseteq U$ indicate the random set sampled from $e$’s
distribution. Since the sampled sets are independent we have$\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\right]=\prod_{e}\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)\right]$. For any $A\subseteq E$ let$f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=&#124;\cup_{e\in A}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)&#124;$ be the number of elements of $U$ covered by the sets sampled from items in $A$.
As in the previous work mentioned above, we assume $f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=n$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$. Therefore we may set $Q=n$. Since the
range of $f$ includes only integers,
we may set $\eta=1$. Applying Theorem [17](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem17 "Theorem 17 ‣ 7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") then yields the following result.

###### Corollary 18

The adaptive greedy algorithm achieves a $(\ln(n)+1)^{2}$-approximation for Stochastic Set Coverage, where $n:=&#124;U&#124;$ is the size of the ground set.

We now provide the proof of Theorem [17](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem17 "Theorem 17 ‣ 7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

Proof of Theorem [17](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem17 "Theorem 17 ‣ 7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"): We will ultimately prove Theorem [17](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem17 "Theorem 17 ‣ 7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") by applying the bound from
Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") for Stochastic Submodular Cover
instances.

The proof mostly consists of justifying this application.
Without loss of generality we may assume$\hat{f}$ is truncated at $Q$, otherwise we may use$\hat{g}(S)=\min\left\{Q,\hat{f}(S)\right\}$ in lieu of $\hat{f}$.
This removes the need to truncate $f$.
Since we established the adaptive submodularity of $f$ in the proof of
Theorem [16](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem16 "Theorem 16 ‣ 6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), and by assumption $f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$,
to apply Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") we need only show
that $f$ is strongly adaptive monotone and strongly adaptive submodular
and that the instances under consideration are self–certifying.

We begin by showing the strong adaptive monotonicity of $f$.
Fix a partial realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, an item $e\notin\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ and a state $o$. Let${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\cup\left\{(e,o)\right\}$.
Then treating ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ as subsets of $E\times O$, and
using the monotonicity of $\hat{f}$, we obtain

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right]=\hat{f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\leq\hat{f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}\right],$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

which is equivalent to the strong adaptive monotonicity condition.

Next we show the strong adaptive adaptive submodularity of $f$ by showing it is
pointwise submodular (having already proven adaptive submodularity for it).
This is clearly true, since for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$,$S\mapsto\hat{f}(\left\{(e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)):e\in S\right\})$ is monotone submodular
by assumption.

Finally we prove that these instances are self–certifying.
Consider any ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}$ consistent with ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
Then

|  | $f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=\hat{f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})=f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}).$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

Since $f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})=Q$ by assumption,
it follows that $f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ iff$f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})=f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$, so the instance is self–certifying.

We have shown that $f$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ satisfy the assumptions of
Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") on this self–certifying instance. Hence we may apply it
to obtain the claimed approximation guarantee. $\blacksquare$

## 8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing

For our next application, consider the following scenario.
Suppose we would like to generate demand for a genuinely novel
product. Potential customers do not realize how valuable the new
product will be to them, and conventional advertisements are
failing to convince them to try it.
In this case, we may try to spur demand by offering a special
promotional deal to a
select few people, and
hope that demand builds virally, propagating through the social
network as people recommend the product to their friends and
associates.
Supposing we know something about the structure of the
social networks people inhabit, and how ideas, innovation, and new
product adoption diffuse through them, this begs the
question: to which initial set of people should we offer the
promotional deal, in order to spur maximum demand for our product?

This, broadly, is the viral marketing problem.
The same problem arises in the context of spreading technological,
cultural, and intellectual innovations, broadly construed. In the
interest of unified terminology we
follow [Kempe et al. (2003)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib39) and
talk of spreading *influence* through the social network, where
we say people are *active* if they have adopted the idea or
innovation in question, and *inactive* otherwise, and that $a$ *influences* $b$ if $a$ convinces $b$ to adopt the idea or innovation in question.

There are many ways to model the diffusion dynamics governing the
spread of influence in a social network. We consider a basic and
well-studied model, the *independent
cascade model*, described in detail below.
For this model [Kempe et al. (2003)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib39) obtain a very interesting result;
they show that the eventual spread of the influence ${f}$ (i.e., the
ultimate number of customers that demand the product) is a monotone
submodular function of the seed set $S$ of people initially selected. This, in
conjunction with the results of [Nemhauser et al. (1978)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib54) implies that the
greedy algorithm
obtains at least $\left({1-\frac{1}{e}}\right)$ of the value of the best
feasible seed set of size at most $k$, i.e., $\mathop{\rm arg\,max}_{S:&#124;S&#124;\leq k}{f}(S)$,
where we interpret $k$ as the budget for the promotional campaign.
Though [Kempe et al.](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib39) consider only the maximum coverage version of
the viral marketing problem, their result
in conjunction with that of [Wolsey (1982)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib66) also implies that
the greedy algorithm will obtain a quota $Q$ of value at a cost of
at most $\ln(Q)+1$ times the cost of the optimal set$\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{S}\left\{c(S):f(S)\geq Q\right\}$ if $f$ takes on only integral
values.

*Figure 3: Illustration of the Adaptive Viral Marketing problem. Left:
the underlying social network.
Middle: the people influenced and
the observations obtained after one person is selected.*

### 8.1 Adaptive Viral Marketing

The viral marketing problem has a very natural adaptive analog. Instead of selecting a fixed set of people in advance, we may select a person to offer the promotion to,
make some observations about the resulting spread of demand for our product, and repeat. See Fig. [3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") for an illustration.
In §[8.2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8.SS2 "8.2 Guarantees for the Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"),
we use the idea of adaptive submodularity to
obtain results analogous to
those of [Kempe et al. (2003)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib39) in the adaptive setting.
Specifically, we show that the greedy policy obtains at least $\left({1-\frac{1}{e}}\right)$ of the value of
the best *policy*. Moreover, we extend this result by
achieving that guarantee not only
for the case where our reward is simply the number of influenced
people, but also for any (nonnegative) monotone submodular function of the *set* of people influenced. In §[8.3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8.SS3 "8.3 The Minimum Cost Cover Objective ‣ 8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") we consider the minimum cost
cover objective, and show that the greedy policy obtains a squared logarithmic
approximation for it. To our knowledge, no approximation results for this adaptive variant of the viral marketing problem have been known.

#### 8.1.1 Independent Cascade Model

In this model, the social network is a directed graph $G=(V,A)$ where each vertex in $V$ is a person, and each edge $(u,v)\in A$ has an
associated binary random variable $X_{uv}$ indicating if $u$ will
influence $v$. That is,$X_{uv}=1$ if $u$ will influence$v$ once it has been influenced, and $X_{uv}=0$ otherwise.
The random variables $X_{uv}$ are independent, and have known means$p_{uv}:=\mathbb{E}\left[X_{uv}\right]$.
We will call an edge $(u,v)$ with $X_{uv}=1$ a *live edge* and
an edge with $X_{uv}=0$ a *dead edge*.
When a node $u$ is activated, the edges $X_{uv}$ to each neighbor $v$ of $u$ are sampled, and $v$ is activated if $(u,v)$ is live.
Influence can then spread from $u$’s neighbors to their neighbors, and so on,
according to the same process. Once active, nodes remain active
throughout the process, however [Kempe et al. (2003)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib39) show that this
assumption is without loss of generality, and can be removed.

#### 8.1.2 The Feedback Model

In the Adaptive Viral Marketing problem under the
independent cascades model, the items correspond to people we can
activate by offering them the promotional deal. How we define the
states ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(u)$ depends on what information we obtain as a result
of activating $u$. Given the nature of the diffusion process,
activating $u$ can have wide-ranging effects, so the state${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(u)$ has more to do with the state of the social network on the
whole than with $u$ in particular.
Specifically, we model${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(u)$ as a function ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{u}:A\to\left\{0,1,?\right\}$, where${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{u}((u,v))=0$ means that activating $u$ has revealed that $(u,v)$ is dead,${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{u}((u,v))=1$ means that activating $u$ has revealed that $(u,v)$ is live, and${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{u}((u,v))=\ ?$ means that activating $u$ has not revealed the
status of $(u,v)$ (i.e., the value of $X_{uv}$). We require each
realization to be *consistent* and *complete*.
Consistency means that no edge should be declared both live and dead
by any two states. That is, for all$u,v\in V$ and $a\in A$, $({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{u}(a),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{v}(a))\notin\left\{(0,1),(1,0)\right\}$.
Completeness means that the status of each edge is revealed by some
activation. That is, for all$a\in A$ there exists $u\in V$ such that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{u}(a)\in\left\{0,1\right\}$.
A consistent and complete realization thus encodes $X_{uv}$ for each
edge $(u,v)$. Let ${A({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})}$ denote the live edges as encoded by${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
There are several candidates for which edge sets we
are allowed to observe when activating a node $u$.
Here we consider what we call the *Full-Adoption Feedback Model*:
After activating $u$ we get to
see the status (live or dead) of all edges exiting $v$, for all
nodes $v$ reachable from $u$ via live edges (i.e., reachable from$u$ in $(V,{A({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})})$, where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ is the true realization.
We illustrate the full-adoption feedback model in Fig. [3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

#### 8.1.3 The Objective Function

In the simplest case, the reward for influencing a set $U\subseteq V$ of nodes is $\hat{f}(U):=&#124;U&#124;$. [Kempe et al. (2003)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib39) obtain an $\left({1-\frac{1}{e}}\right)$-approximation for the slightly more general case in which
each node $u$ has a weight $w_{u}$ indicating its importance,
and the reward is $\hat{f}(U):=\sum_{u\in U}w_{u}$. We
generalize this result further, to include arbitrary nonnegative
monotone submodular reward functions $\hat{f}$.
This allows us, for example, to encode a value associated with the *diversity* of the set of nodes influenced, such as the notion
that it is better to achieve $20\%$ market penetration in five
different (equally important) demographic segments than $100\%$ market penetration in one
and $0\%$ in the others.

### 8.2 Guarantees for the Maximum Coverage Objective

We are now ready to formally state our result for the maximum coverage
objective.

###### Theorem 19

The greedy policy $\pi^{\text{greedy}}$ obtains at least $\left({1-\frac{1}{e}}\right)$ of the value of
the best *policy* for the Adaptive Viral Marketing problem with
arbitrary monotone submodular reward functions,
in the independent cascade and
full-adoption feedback models
discussed above.
That is, if $\sigma(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is the set of all activated nodes
when $S$ is the seed set of activated nodes and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ is the realization, $\hat{f}:2^{V}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is an
arbitrary monotone submodular function indicating the reward for
influencing a set, and the objective function is$f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=\hat{f}(\sigma(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}))$, then for all policies$\pi$ and all $k\in\mathbb{N}$ we have

|  | $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{\text{greedy}}_{[{k}]})\geq\left({1-\frac{1}{e}}\right)f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{k}]}).$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

More generally, if $\pi$ is an $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy
then $\forall\ell\in\mathbb{N}$, $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}]})\geq\left({1-e^{-\ell/\alpha k}}\right)f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{*}_{[{k}]})$.

Proof  Adaptive monotonicity follows immediately from the fact that $f(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is monotonic for each ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
It thus suffices to prove that $f$ is adaptive submodular with respect to the
probability distribution on realizations ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$,
because then we can invoke
Theorem [5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 5 ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") to complete the proof.

We will say we have *observed* an edge $(u,v)$ if we know its
status, i.e., if it is live or dead.
Fix any ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ such that${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ and any $v\notin\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$.
We must show $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{v}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)\leq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{v}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$.
To prove this rigorously,
we define a coupled distribution $\mu$ over pairs of realizations ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$. Note that given the feedback model,
the realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ is a function
of the random variables $\left\{X_{uw}:(u,w)\in A\right\}$ indicating the status of each edge. For conciseness we use
the notation $\mathbf{X}=\left\{X_{uw}:(u,w)\in A\right\}$.
We define $\mu$ implicitly in terms of a
joint distribution $\hat{\mu}$ on $\mathbf{X}\times\mathbf{X}^{\prime}$,
where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(\mathbf{X})$ and${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}(\mathbf{X}^{\prime})$ are the realizations
induced by the two distinct sets of random edge statuses, respectively.
Hence $\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(\mathbf{X}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(\mathbf{X}^{\prime}))=\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}^{\prime})$.
Next, let us say a partial realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ observes an edge $e$ if
some $w\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ has revealed its status as being live or
dead.
For edges $(u,w)$ observed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, the random variable$X_{uw}$ is deterministically set to the status observed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
Similarly, for edges $(u,w)$ observed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$, the random variable$X^{\prime}_{uw}$ is deterministically set to the status observed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$.
Note that since ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$, the
state of all edges which are
observed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ are the same in ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}$.
All $(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}^{\prime})\in\operatorname{support}(\hat{\mu})$ have these properties.
Additionally, we will construct $\hat{\mu}$ so that
the status of all edges which are
unobserved by both ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ are the same in $\mathbf{X}$ and $\mathbf{X}^{\prime}$, meaning$X_{uw}=X^{\prime}_{uw}$ for all such edges $(u,w)$, or else $\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}^{\prime})=0$.

The above constraints leave us with the following degrees of freedom:
we may select $X_{uw}$ for all $(u,w)\in A$ which are unobserved by${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$. We select them independently, such that $\mathbb{E}\left[X_{uw}\right]=p_{uw}$ as with the prior ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$. Hence for all $(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}^{\prime})$ satisfying the above constraints,

|  | $\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}^{\prime})=\prod_{(u,w)\text{ unobserved by }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}p_{uw}^{X_{uw}}\left({1-p_{uw}}\right)^{1-X_{uw}},$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

and otherwise $\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}^{\prime})=0$.
Note that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)}}=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}}\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}\right)}}=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$.
We next claim that for all$({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})\in\operatorname{support}(\mu)$

|  | $\displaystyle f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})\cup\left\{v\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$ | $\displaystyle\leq$ | $\displaystyle f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{v\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}).\quad$ |  | (19) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | ------------------------------ | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Recall $f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=\hat{f}(\sigma(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}))$, where $\sigma(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is the set of all activated nodes
when $S$ is the seed set of activated nodes and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ is the realization.
Let $B=\sigma(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ and $C=\sigma(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{v\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ denote the active nodes before and after selecting $v$ after $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ under realizations${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, and similarly define $B^{\prime}$ and $C^{\prime}$ with respect to ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}$.
Let $D:=C\setminus B$, $D^{\prime}:=C^{\prime}\setminus B^{\prime}$.
Then Eq. ([19](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8.E19 "In 8.2 Guarantees for the Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) is equivalent to $\hat{f}(B^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime})-\hat{f}(B^{\prime})\leq\hat{f}(B\cup D)-\hat{f}(B)$.
By the submodularity of $\hat{f}$, it suffices to show that $B\subseteq B^{\prime}$ and $D^{\prime}\subseteq D$ to prove the above inequality,
which we will now do.

We start by proving $B\subseteq B^{\prime}$. Fix $w\in B$. Then there
exists a path from some $u\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ to $w$ in$(V,{A({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})})$. Moreover, every edge in this path is not only live
but also observed to be live,
by definition of the feedback model. Since$({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})\in\operatorname{support}(\mu)$, this implies that every edge
in this path is also live under ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}$, as edges observed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ must have the same status under both ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}$.
It follows that there is
a path from $u$ to $w$ in $(V,{A({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}})})$.
Since $u$ is clearly also in $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$, we conclude $w\in B^{\prime}$,
hence $B\subseteq B^{\prime}$.

Next we show $D^{\prime}\subseteq D$. Fix some $w\in D^{\prime}$ and suppose by way of contradiction that $w\notin D$. Hence there exists a path $P$ from $v$ to $w$ in $(V,{A({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}})})$ but no such path exists in $(V,{A({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})})$. The
edges of $P$ are all live under ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}$, and at least one must be
dead under ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$. Let $(u,u^{\prime})$ be such an edge in $P$. Because
the status of this edge differs in ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}$, and$({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})\in\operatorname{support}(\mu)$, it must be that $(u,u^{\prime})$ is
observed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ but not observed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
Because it is observed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$, in our feedback model it must be that $u$ is active
after $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$ is selected, i.e., $u\in B^{\prime}$.
However, this implies that all nodes reachable from $u$ via edges in$P$ are also active after $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$ is selected, since all the
edges in $P$ are live. Hence all such nodes, including $w$, are in$B^{\prime}$. Since $D^{\prime}$ and $B^{\prime}$ are disjoint, this implies $w\notin D^{\prime}$, a
contradiction.

Having proved Eq. ([19](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8.E19 "In 8.2 Guarantees for the Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), we now proceed to use it to show$\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{v}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)\leq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{v}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ as in
§[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

|  | $\begin{array}[]{lllll}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{v}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)&=&\sum_{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})}\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})\left({f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})\cup\left\{v\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})}\right)&&\\[8.53581pt] &\leq&\sum_{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})}\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})\left({f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{v\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})}\right)&=&\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{v}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\end{array}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

which completes the proof.

#### 8.2.1 Comparison with Stochastic Submodular Maximization

It is worth contrasting the Adaptive Viral Marketing problem with the
Stochastic Submodular Maximization problem
of §[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). In the latter problem, we
can think of the items as being
random independently distributed sets.
In Adaptive Viral Marketing by contrast, the random sets (of nodes
influenced when a fixed node is selected) depend on the random status
of the edges, and hence may be correlated through them.
Nevertheless, we can obtain the same $\left({1-\frac{1}{e}}\right)$ approximation factor for both problems.

##### A Comment on the Myopic Feedback Model.

In the conference version of this
article ([Golovin and Krause, 2010](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib22)), we considered an alternate feedback
model called the *myopic feedback* model, in which after
activating $v$ we see the status of all edges exiting $v$ in the
social network, i.e., $\partial_{+}(u):=\left\{(u,v):v\in V\right\}\cap A$. We claimed that the objective $f$ as defined previously is adaptive submodular in the independent cascade model with myopic feedback, and
hence the greedy policy obtains a $(1-\frac{1}{e})$ approximation for it.
We hereby retract this claim, and furthermore give a counterexample demonstrating
that $f$ is not adaptive submodular under myopic feedback.

Consider a graph $G=(V,E)$ with vertices$V:=\left\{u,v,w\right\}$, and edges$E:=\left\{(u,v),(v,w)\right\}$.
The edge parameters are $p_{uv}=1$ and $p_{vw}=1-\epsilon$.
Let $\hat{f}(U)=&#124;U&#124;$ and construct $f$ from $\hat{f}$ accordingly.
We let ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}=\left\{(u,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{u})\right\}$, where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{u}((u,v))=1$ and${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{u}((v,w))=\ ?$.
Let ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}=\left\{(u,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{u}),(v,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{v})\right\}$ where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{v}((v,w))=0$.
Clearly, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subset{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$.
Note $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{w}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)=\epsilon$, since the marginal benefit of$w$ over $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ is one if $(v,w)$ is dead, and zero if it
is live, and the former occurs with probability $\epsilon$.
In contrast, $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{w}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)=1$, since ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ contains the
observation that $(v,w)$ is dead.
Hence $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{w}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)<\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{w}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)$, which violates adaptive submodularity.
However, we conjecture that the greedy policy still obtains a constant
factor approximation even in the myopic feedback model.

### 8.3 The Minimum Cost Cover Objective

We may also wish to adaptively run our campaign until a certain level of market penetration has been achieved, e.g., a certain number of people have adopted the product. We can formalize this goal using the minimum cost cover objective.
For this objective, we have an instance of Adaptive
Stochastic Minimum Cost Cover, in which we are given a quota $Q\leq\hat{f}(V)$ (quantifying the desired level of market penetration) and
we must adaptively select nodes to activate until the set of all active nodes $S$ satisfies$\hat{f}(S)\geq Q$. We obtain the following result.

###### Theorem 20

Fix a monotone submodular function $\hat{f}:2^{V}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ indicating
the reward for
influencing a set, and a quota $Q\leq\hat{f}(V)$.
Suppose the objective is$f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=\min\left\{Q,\hat{f}(\sigma(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}))\right\}$, where$\sigma(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is the set of all activated nodes
when $S$ is the seed set of activated nodes and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ is the realization.
Let $\eta$ be any value such that$\hat{f}(S)>Q-\eta$ implies $\hat{f}(S)\geq Q$ for all $S$.
Then any $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy $\pi$ on average costs at most$\alpha\left({\ln\left({\frac{Q}{\eta}}\right)+1}\right)^{2}$ times the average cost of
the best *policy* obtaining $Q$ reward
for the Adaptive Viral Marketing problem
in the independent cascade model with
full-adoption feedback as
described above.
That is,${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\leq\alpha\left({\ln\left({\frac{Q}{\eta}}\right)+1}\right)^{2}{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi^{*})}}$ for any $\pi^{*}$ that covers every realization.

Proof  We prove Theorem [20](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem20 "Theorem 20 ‣ 8.3 The Minimum Cost Cover Objective ‣ 8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") by recourse to
Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").
We have already established that $f$ is adaptive submodular, in the proof
of Theorem [19](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem19 "Theorem 19 ‣ 8.2 Guarantees for the Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). It remains to show that $f$ is
strongly adaptive monotone and strongly adaptive submodular,
that these instances are self–certifying, and
that $Q$ and $\eta$ equal the corresponding terms
in the statement of Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

We start with strong adaptive monotonicity. Fix ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, $e\notin\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$, and $o\in O$. We must show

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)=o\right].$ |  | (20) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Let $V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)$ denote the active nodes after selecting$\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ and observing ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
By definition of the full adoption feedback model,$V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)$ consists of precisely
those nodes $v$ for which there exists a path $P_{uv}$ from some $u\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ to $v$ via exclusively live edges. The edges whose
status we observe consist of all edges exiting nodes in$V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)$.
It follows that every path
from any $u\in V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)$ to any $v\in V\setminus V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)$ contains at least one edge which is observed by${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ to
be dead.
Hence, in every ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, the set of nodes activated by
selecting $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ is the same.
Therefore $\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]=\hat{f}(V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right))$.
Similarly, if we define ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\cup\left\{(e,o)\right\}$, then$\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)=o\right]=\hat{f}(V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}\right))$.
Note that once activated, nodes never become inactive.
Hence, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ implies$V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)\subseteq V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}\right)$.
Since $\hat{f}$ is monotone by assumption, this means$\hat{f}(V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right))\leq\hat{f}(V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}\right))$ which implies Eq. ([20](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8.E20 "In 8.3 The Minimum Cost Cover Objective ‣ 8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) and strong adaptive monotonicity.

Next we establish strong adaptive submodularity. Given that we have already
established adaptive submodularity, it is sufficient to also prove pointwise
submodularity. For a fixed realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ we have a set of live edges$\left\{(u,v):X_{uv}=1\right\}$ which induce a set system in which $u$ covers
all nodes reachable from $u$ via live edges.
Let $S_{u}$ denote this set.
It is straightforward to verify that a monotone submodular function $\hat{f}$ on
nodes induces a monotone submodular function on sets of those nodes.
That is,

|  | $g(A):=\hat{f}\left({\cup_{u\in A}S_{u}}\right)$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

is submodular whenever $\hat{f}$ is.
In particular, $g$ is submodular if for every $A$ and $B$ we have$g(A)+g(B)\geq g(A\cap B)+g(A\cup B)$ however one can easily verify that
this set of constraints is a subset of the corresponding submodularity
constaints on $\hat{f}$.

Next we establish that these instances are self–certifying.
Note that for every ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ we have$f(V,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=\min\left\{Q,\hat{f}(V)\right\}=Q$.
From our earlier remarks, we know that$f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=\hat{f}(V^{+}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right))$ for every ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
Hence for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}$ consistent with ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, we
have $f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$ and so$f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q$ if and only if $f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})=Q$,
which proves that the instance is self–certifying.

Finally we show that $Q$ and $\eta$ equal the corresponding terms
