in the statement of Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").
As noted earlier, $f(V,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=Q$ for all${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$. We defined $\eta$ as some value such that$\hat{f}(S)>Q-\eta$ implies $\hat{f}(S)\geq Q$ for all $S$.
Since $\operatorname{range}(f)=\left\{\min\left\{Q,\hat{f}(S)\right\}:S\subseteq V\right\}$, it
follows that we cannot have$f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})\in(Q-\eta,Q)$ for any $S$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, so that $\eta$ satisfies the requirements of the corresponding term in
Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). Hence we may apply
Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") on this self–certifying instance with $Q$ and $\eta$ to obtain the claimed result.

## 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning

An important problem in AI is automated diagnosis. For example, suppose we have different hypotheses about the state of a patient, and can run medical tests to rule out inconsistent hypotheses. The goal is to adaptively choose tests to infer the state of the patient as quickly as possible.

A similar problem arises in active learning. Obtaining labeled data to train a classifier is typically expensive, as it often involves asking an expert. In active learning (*c.f.* [Cohn et al. (1996)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib8); [McCallum and Nigam (1998)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib50)), the key idea is that some labels are more informative than others: labeling a few unlabeled examples can imply the labels of many other unlabeled examples, and thus the cost of obtaining the labels from an expert can be avoided. As is standard, we assume that we are given a set of hypotheses $H$,
and a set of unlabeled data points $X$ where each $x\in X$ is independently drawn from some
distribution $\mathcal{D}$. Let $L$ be the set of possible labels.
Classical learning theory yields *probably approximately correct* (PAC) bounds, bounding the number $n$ of examples drawn i.i.d. from $\mathcal{D}$ needed to output a hypothesis $h$ that will have expected error at most $\varepsilon$ with probability at least $1-\delta$,
for some fixed $\varepsilon,\delta>0$. That is, if $h^{*}$ is the
target hypothesis (with zero error), and$\operatorname{error}(h):=\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[h(x)\neq h^{*}(x)\right]$ is the error of $h$,
we require$\mathbb{P}\left[\operatorname{error}(h)\leq\varepsilon\right]\geq 1-\delta$.
The latter probability is taken with respect to $\mathcal{D}(X)$; the learned
hypothesis $h$ and thus $\operatorname{error}(h)$ depend on it.
A key challenge in active learning is to avoid bias: actively selected examples are no longer i.i.d., and thus sample complexity bounds for passive learning no longer apply. If one is not careful, active learning may require more samples than passive learning to achieve the same generalization error.
One natural approach to active learning that is guaranteed to perform at least as well as passive learning is *pool-based active learning* ([McCallum and Nigam, 1998](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib50)): The idea is to draw $n$ *unlabeled* examples i.i.d. However, instead of obtaining all labels, labels are adaptively requested until the labels of all unlabeled examples are implied by the obtained labels. Now we have obtained $n$ labeled examples drawn i.i.d., and classical PAC bounds still apply. The key question is how to request the labels for the pool to infer the remaining labels as quickly as possible.

In the case of binary labels (or test outcomes) $L=\left\{-1,1\right\}$, various authors have considered greedy
policies which generalize binary
search ([Garey and Graham, 1974](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib19); [Loveland, 1985](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib49); [Arkin et al., 1993](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib2); [Kosaraju et al., 1999](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib40); [Dasgupta, 2004](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib10); [Guillory and Bilmes, 2009](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib32); [Nowak, 2009](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib55)).
The simplest of these, called *generalized binary search* (GBS) or the *splitting algorithm*, works as follows.
Define the *version
space* $V$ to be the set of hypotheses consistent with the observed
labels (here we assume that there is no label noise).
In the worst-case setting, GBS selects a query $x\in X$ that minimizes $\left&#124;\sum_{h\in V}h(x)\right&#124;$.
In the Bayesian setting we assume we are given a prior $p_{H}$ over
hypotheses; in this case GBS selects a query $x\in X$ that minimizes $\left&#124;\sum_{h\in V}p_{H}(h)\cdot h(x)\right&#124;$. Intuitively
these policies myopically attempt to shrink a measure of the version space (i.e.,
the cardinality or the probability mass) as quickly as
possible.
The former provides an ${\mathcal{O}}(\log&#124;H&#124;)$-approximation for the
worst-case number of queries ([Arkin et al., 1993](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib2)), and the latter provides an${\mathcal{O}}(\log\frac{1}{\min_{h}p_{H}(h)})$-approximation for the
expected number of queries ([Kosaraju et al., 1999](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib40); [Dasgupta, 2004](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib10)) and a natural
generalization of GBS obtains the same guarantees with a larger set of
labels ([Guillory and Bilmes, 2009](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib32)).
Kosaraju et al. also prove that running GBS on a modified prior$p_{H}^{\prime}(h)\propto\max\left\{p_{H}(h),1/&#124;H&#124;^{2}\log&#124;H&#124;\right\}$ is sufficient to obtain an ${\mathcal{O}}(\log&#124;H&#124;)$-approximation.

*Figure 4: Illustration of the Active Learning problem, in the simple
special case of one-dimensional data and
binary threshold hypotheses $H=\left\{h_{\tau}:\tau\in\mathbb{R}\right\}$, where $h_{\tau}(x)=1$ if $x\geq\tau$ and $0$ otherwise.*

Viewed from this perspective of the previous sections, shrinking the
version space amounts to “covering” all false hypotheses with stochastic
sets (i.e., queries), where query $x$ covers all hypotheses that
disagree with the target hypothesis $h^{*}$ at $x$. That is,$x$ covers $\left\{h:h(x)\neq h^{*}(x)\right\}$. As
in §[8](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S8 "8 Application: Adaptive Viral Marketing ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), these sets may be correlated in
complex ways determined by the set of possible hypotheses.
As we will show, the reduction in version space mass is adaptive submodular, and this allows us to obtain
a new analysis of GBS using adaptive submodularity,
which has a weaker approximation guarantee than is optimal, but
is arguably more amenable to extensions
and generalizations than previous analyses.

###### Theorem 21

In the Bayesian setting in which there is a prior $p_{H}$ on a
finite set of hypotheses $H$, the generalized binary search algorithm
makes $\textsf{OPT}\cdot\left({\ln\left({\frac{1}{\min_{h}p_{H}(h)}}\right)+1}\right)^{2}$ queries in
expectation to identify a hypothesis drawn from $p_{H}$,
where OPT is the minimum expected number of queries
made by any policy.
If $\min_{h}p_{H}(h)$ is sufficiently small,
running the algorithm on a modified prior$p_{H}^{\prime}(h)\propto\max\left\{p_{H}(h),1/&#124;H&#124;^{2}\right\}$ improves the approximation factor to${\mathcal{O}}\left({\left({\ln&#124;H&#124;}\right)^{2}}\right)$.

We devote the better part of the remainder of this section to the
proof of Theorem [21](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem21 "Theorem 21 ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), which has several
components.
We first address the important special case of a uniform prior
over hypotheses, i.e., $p_{H}(h)=1/&#124;H&#124;$ for all $h\in H$, and then we reduce the case with a general prior to a
uniform prior.
We wish to appeal to Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"),
so we convert the problem into an Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover problem.

### 9.1 The Reduction to Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover

Define a realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h}$ for each hypothesis $h\in H$.
The ground set is $E=X$, and the
outcomes are binary; we define $O=\left\{-1,1\right\}$ instead of
using $\left\{0,1\right\}$ to be consistent with our earlier exposition.
For all $h\in H$ we set ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h}\equiv h$, meaning${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h}(x)=h(x)$ for all $x\in X$.
To define the objective function, we first need some notation.
Given observed labels ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subset X\times O$, let$V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ denote the version space, i.e., the set of hypotheses for
which $h(x)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}(x)$ for all $x\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$.
See Fig. [4](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") for an illustration of an active
learning problem in the case of
indicator hypotheses.
For a set of hypotheses $V$, let $p_{H}(V):=\sum_{h\in V}p_{H}(h)$ denote their total prior probability.
Finally, let ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}(S,h)=\left\{(x,h(x))\ :\ x\in S\right\}$ be the function with domain $S$ that agrees with $h$ on $S$.
We define the objective function by

|  | $f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h})\ :=\ 1-p_{H}(V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}(S,h)))\ =\ p_{H}\!\left({\left\{h^{\prime}\ :\ \exists x\in S,h^{\prime}(x)\neq h(x)\right\}}\right)$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

and use ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h}}\right)}}=p_{H}(h)=1/&#124;H&#124;$ for all $h$.
Let $\pi^{*}$ be an optimal policy for this
Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover instance. Note that there is an exact correspondence between
policies for the original problem of finding the target hypothesis and
our problem of covering the true realization; $h^{*}$ is identified as the target
hypothesis if and only if the version space is reduced to$\left\{h^{*}\right\}$ which occurs if and only if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h^{*}}$ is covered.
Hence ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi^{*})}}=\textsf{OPT}$.
Note that because we have assumed a uniform prior over hypotheses, we have$f(X,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h})=1-1/&#124;H&#124;$ for all $h$.
Furthermore, the instances are self–certifying.

###### Lemma 22

The instances described above are self–certifying for arbitrary priors $p_{H}$.

Proof  Intuitively, theses instances are self–certifying because to cover ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h^{*}}$ a
policy must identify ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h^{*}}$. More formally, these instances are self–certifying because for any${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ such that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, we have that$f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h})=f(X,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h})$ implies$V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})=\left\{h\right\}$. This in turn means that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h}$ is the *only* realization consistent with ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, which trivially implies that any
realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ also has $f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})=f(X,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}^{\prime})$; hence the instance is self–certifying.

### 9.2 The Uniform Prior

We next prove that the instances
generated are adaptive submodular and strongly adaptive
monotone under a uniform prior.

###### Lemma 23

In the instances described above, $f$ is strongly adaptive
monotone and strongly adaptive submodular and with respect to a
uniform prior $p_{H}$.

Proof  Demonstrating strong adaptive monotonicity under a uniform prior
amounts to proving that adding labels
cannot grow the version space, which is clear in our model.
That is, each query $x$ eliminates some subset of
hypotheses, and as more queries are performed, the subset of
hypotheses eliminated by $x$ cannot grow.
Moving on to adaptive submodularity, consider the expected marginal contribution of $x$ under two partial realizations ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is a
subrealization of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ (i.e., ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subset{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$), and $x\notin\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})$.
Let ${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\!\left[{x}/{o}\right]$ be the partial
realization with domain $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{x\right\}$ that agrees with${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ on its domain, and maps $x$ to $o$.
For each $o\in O$,
let$a_{o}:=p_{H}(V({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\!\left[{x}/{o}\right]))$, $b_{o}:=p_{H}(V({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}\!\left[{x}/{o}\right]))$.
Since a hypothesis eliminated from the version space cannot later
appear in the version space, we have$a_{o}\geq b_{o}$ for all $o$.
Next, note the expected reduction in version space mass (and hence the expected
marginal contribution) due to selecting $x$ given partial realization${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is

|  | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{x}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)=\sum_{o\in O}a_{o}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(x)\neq o\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]=\sum_{o}a_{o}\left({\frac{\sum_{o^{\prime}\neq o}a_{o^{\prime}}}{\sum_{o^{\prime}}a_{o^{\prime}}}}\right)=\frac{\sum_{o\neq o^{\prime}}a_{o}a_{o^{\prime}}}{\sum_{o^{\prime}}a_{o^{\prime}}}.$ |  | (21) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

The corresponding quantity for ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ has $b_{o}$ substituted
for $a_{o}$ in Eq. ([21](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9.E21 "In 9.2 The Uniform Prior ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), for each $o\in O$.
To prove adaptive submodularity
we must show$\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{x}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\geq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{x}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}}}\right)$ and to do so it suffices to show that $\partial\phi/\partial z_{o}\geq 0$ for each $o$ and$\vec{z}\in\left\{\vec{c}\in[0,1]^{O}:\sum_{o}c_{o}>0\right\}$, where$\phi(\vec{z}\,):=\left({\sum_{o\neq o^{\prime}}z_{o}z_{o^{\prime}}}\right)/\left({\sum_{o^{\prime}}z_{o^{\prime}}}\right)$ has the
same functional form as the expression for $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{x}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ in Eq. ([21](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9.E21 "In 9.2 The Uniform Prior ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")).
This is because $\partial\phi/\partial z_{o}\geq 0$ for each $o$ implies that growing the version space
in any manner cannot decrease the expected marginal benefit of query $x$, and
hence shrinking it in any manner cannot increase the expected marginal benefit
of $x$.
It is
indeed the case that $\partial\phi/\partial z_{o}\geq 0$ for each $o$. More specifically, it holds that

|  | $\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial z_{a}}=\frac{\sum_{b\neq a}z_{b}^{2}+\sum_{(b,c):b\neq c,b\neq a,c\neq a}z_{b}z_{c}}{\left({\sum_{b}z_{b}}\right)^{2}}\geq 0,$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

which can be derived through elementary calculus.

To further show strong adaptive submodularity, we prove pointwise submodularity
of the objective. For any fixed realization, $h$, the objective$S\mapsto f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ amounts to a weighted set cover problem on the
incorrect hypotheses (plus a constant), and is thus submodular.

Hence we can apply Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") to
this self–certifying instance with maximum reward threshold $Q=1-1/&#124;H&#124;$, and
minimum gap $\eta=1/&#124;H&#124;$, to obtain an upper bound of$\textsf{OPT}\left({\ln\left({&#124;H&#124;-1}\right)+1}\right)^{2}$ on the number of
queries made by the
generalized binary search algorithm (which corresponds exactly to the greedy policy
for Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover)
under the assumption of a uniform
prior over $H$.

### 9.3 Arbitrary Priors

Now consider general priors over $H$.
We construct the
Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover
instance as before, only we change the objective function to

|  | $f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h})\ :=\ 1-p_{H}(V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}(S,h)))+p_{H}(h)\mbox{.}$ |  | (22) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

First note that the instances remain self–certifying. The proof of Lemma [22](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem22 "Lemma 22 ‣ 9.1 The Reduction to Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") goes through completely unchanged by the modification of $f$. We proceed to
show adaptive submodularity and strong adaptive monotonicity.

###### Lemma 24

The objective function $f$ as described in Eq. ([22](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9.E22 "In 9.3 Arbitrary Priors ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"))
is strongly adaptive monotone and strongly adaptive submodular with
respect to arbitrary priors $p_{H}$.

Proof  The modified objective is still adaptive submodular, because$(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h})\mapsto p_{H}(h)$ is clearly so, and because adaptive submodularity is defined
via linear inequalities it is preserved under taking nonnegative linear combinations.
Note that $f(X,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h})=1$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h}$.
It is still strongly adaptive submodular, because it is still pointwise
submodular.

Showing $f$ is strongly adaptive monotone requires slightly more
work than before.
Fix ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}},x\notin\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$, and$o\in O$. We must show

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{x\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(x)=o\right].$ |  | (23) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Plugging in
the definition of $f$, the
inequality we wish to prove may be simplified to

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[p_{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[p_{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\!\left[{x}/{o}\right]\right]\ \leq\ p_{H}(V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))-p_{H}(V({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\!\left[{x}/{o}\right]))\mbox{.}$ |  | (24) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}$ is the random realization of the hypothesis, and$p_{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{h})=p_{H}(h)$ for all $h$.
Let $V_{\text{elim}}:=V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})-V({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\!\left[{x}/{o}\right])$ be the set of hypotheses
eliminated from the version space by the observation $h(x)=o$.
Rewriting Eq. ([24](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9.E24 "In 9.3 Arbitrary Priors ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), we get

|  | $\sum_{h\in V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})}\frac{p_{H}(h)^{2}}{p_{H}(V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))}\ -\sum_{h\in V({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\!\left[{x}/{o}\right])}\frac{p_{H}(h)^{2}}{p_{H}(V({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\!\left[{x}/{o}\right]))}\ \leq\ p_{H}(V_{\text{elim}})\mbox{.}$ |  | (25) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Let $\operatorname{LHS}_{\ref{eq:active2}}$ denote the left hand side
of Eq. ([25](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9.E25 "In 9.3 Arbitrary Priors ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")). We prove Eq. ([25](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9.E25 "In 9.3 Arbitrary Priors ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) as follows.

|  | $\begin{array}[]{lclr}\operatorname{LHS}_{\ref{eq:active2}}&\leq&\sum_{h\in V_{\text{elim}}}p_{H}(h)^{2}/p_{H}(V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))&[\text{since }p_{H}(V({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\!\left[{x}/{o}\right]))\leq p_{H}(V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))]\\ &\leq&\sum_{h\in V_{\text{elim}}}p_{H}(h)\cdot p_{H}(V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))/p_{H}(V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))&\hskip 0.0pt[\text{since }h\in V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\Rightarrow p_{H}(h)\leq p_{H}(V({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))]\\ &=&p_{H}(V_{\text{elim}})&\\ \end{array}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

We conclude that $f$ is adaptive submodular and strongly
adaptive monotone.

Hence we can apply Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") to
this self–certifying instance
with maximum reward threshold $Q=1$, and
minimum gap $\eta=1/\min_{h}p_{H}(h)$. As a result we obtain an upper bound of$\textsf{OPT}\left({\ln\left({1/\min_{h}p_{H}(h)}\right)+1}\right)^{2}$ on the number of
queries made by
generalized binary search for arbitrary priors, completing the proof
of Theorem [21](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem21 "Theorem 21 ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

### 9.4 Improving the Approximation Factor for Highly Nonuniform Priors

To improve this to an ${\mathcal{O}}\left({\left({\log&#124;H&#124;}\right)^{2}}\right)$-approximation
in the
event that $\min_{h}p_{H}(h)$ is extremely small
using the
observation of [Kosaraju et al. (1999)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib40), call a policy $\pi$ *progressive* if it eliminates at least one hypothesis from its
version space in each query. Let$p_{H}^{\prime}(h)=\max\left\{p_{H}(h),1/&#124;H&#124;^{2}\right\}/Z$ be the modified prior, where $Z:=\sum_{h^{\prime}}\max\left\{p_{H}(h^{\prime}),1/&#124;H&#124;^{2}\right\}$ is the normalizing constant.
Let $c(\pi,h)$ be the cost (i.e., # of queries) of $\pi$ under target $h$. Then${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p)}}:=\sum_{h}c(\pi,h)p(h)$ is the expected cost of $\pi$ under prior $p$.
We will show that ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p_{H}^{\prime})}}$ is a good approximation to${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p_{H})}}$.
Call $h$ *rare* if $p_{H}(h)<1/&#124;H&#124;^{2}$, and *common* otherwise.
First, note that $\sum_{h^{\prime}}\max\left\{p_{H}(h^{\prime}),1/&#124;H&#124;^{2}\right\}\leq 1+1/&#124;H&#124;$, and so$p_{H}^{\prime}(h)\geq\frac{&#124;H&#124;}{&#124;H&#124;+1}p_{H}(h)$,
for all $h$. Hence for all $\pi$, we have${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p_{H}^{\prime})}}\geq\frac{&#124;H&#124;}{&#124;H&#124;+1}{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p_{H})}}$.
Next, we show ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p_{H}^{\prime})}}\leq{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p_{H})}}+1$.
Consider the quantity ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p_{H}^{\prime})}}-{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p_{H})}}=\sum_{h}c(\pi,h)\left({p_{H}^{\prime}(h)-p_{H}(h)}\right)$.
The positive contributions must come from rare hypotheses.
However, the total probability mass of these under $p_{H}^{\prime}$ is at
most $1/&#124;H&#124;$, and since $\pi$ is progressive$c(\pi,h)\leq&#124;H&#124;$ for all $h$, hence the difference in costs is at most one.
Let $\alpha:=\left({\ln\left({\frac{1}{\min_{h}p_{H}^{\prime}(h)}}\right)+1}\right)^{2}\leq\left({\ln\left({&#124;H&#124;^{2}+&#124;H&#124;}\right)+1}\right)^{2}$ be the approximation
factor for generalized binary search when run on $p_{H}^{\prime}$.
Let $\pi$ be the policy of generalized binary search, and
let $\pi^{*}$ be an optimal policy under prior $p_{H}$.
Then

|  | ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p_{H})}}\leq\frac{&#124;H&#124;+1}{&#124;H&#124;}\,{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi,p_{H}^{\prime})}}\leq\frac{&#124;H&#124;+1}{&#124;H&#124;}\,\alpha\,{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi^{*},p_{H}^{\prime})}}\leq\frac{&#124;H&#124;+1}{&#124;H&#124;}\,\alpha\,\left({{c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi^{*},p_{H})}}+1}\right).$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

Thus for a general prior a simple modification of GBS
yields an ${\mathcal{O}}\left({\left({\log&#124;H&#124;}\right)^{2}}\right)$-approximation.

### 9.5 Extensions to Arbitrary Costs, Multiple Classes, and Approximate Greedy Policies

This result easily generalizes to handle
the setting of multiple classes / test outcomes (i.e., $&#124;O&#124;\geq 2$), and$\alpha$-approximate greedy policies, where we lose a factor of$\alpha$ in the approximation factor.
As we describe
in the Appendix,
we can
generalize adaptive submodularity to incorporate costs on items,
which allows us to extend this result to handle query costs as well.
Recently, [Gupta et al. (2010)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib36) showed how to simultaneously remove the
dependence on both costs and probabilities from the approximation ratio.
Specifically, within the context of studying an adaptive travelling
salesman problem
they investigated the *Optimal Decision Tree* problem, which is
equivalent to the active learning problem we consider here. Using a clever, more
complex algorithm than adaptive greedy,
they achieve an ${\mathcal{O}}\left({\log&#124;H&#124;}\right)$-approximation
in the case of non-uniform costs and general priors.

### 9.6 Extensions to Active Learning with Noisy Observations

Theorem [21](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem21 "Theorem 21 ‣ 9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") and the extensions mentioned so far
are in the noise free case, i.e., the result of query $x$ and observes$h^{*}(x)$, where $h^{*}$ is the target hypothesis.
Many practical problems may have *noisy* observations. [Nowak (2009)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib55) considered the case in which the outcomes are binary,
i.e., $O=\left\{-1,1\right\}$, the same query may be asked multiple
times, and for each instance of each query the noise is independent.
In this case he gives performance guarantees for generalized binary
search. While this setting may be appropriate if the noise is due to
measurement error, in some applications the noise is *persistent*, i.e., if query $x$ is asked several times, the observation
is always the same. Recently, [Golovin et al. (2010)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib26) and [Bellala and Scott (2010)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib4) have used the adaptive submodularity framework to obtain
the first algorithms with provable (squared–logarithmic) approximation guarantees for
active learning with persistent noise.

## 10 Experiments

Greedy algorithms are often straightforward to develop and implement,
which explains their popular use in practical applications, such as
Bayesian experimental design and Active Learning, as discussed in
§[9](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S9 "9 Application: Automated Diagnosis and Active Learning ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (also see the excellent introduction of [Nowak (2009)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib55))
and Adaptive Stochastic Set Cover, e.g., for filter design in streaming databases as discussed in §[7](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S7 "7 Application: Stochastic Submodular Coverage ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").
Besides allowing us to prove approximation guarantees for such algorithms, adaptive submodularity provides the following immediate practical benefits:

- 1\.

  The ability to use lazy evaluations to speed up its execution.

- 2\.

  The ability to generate data-dependent bounds on the optimal value.

In this section, we empirically evaluate their benefits within a sensor selection application, in a setting similar to the one described by [Deshpande et al. (2004)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib13).
In this application, we have deployed a network $V$ of wireless sensors, e.g., to monitor temperature in a building or traffic in a road network. Since sensors are battery constrained, we must adaptively select $k$ sensors, and then, given those sensor readings, predict, e.g., the temperature at all remaining locations. This prediction is possible since temperature measurements will typically be correlated across space. Here, we will consider the case where sensors can fail to report measurements due to hardware failures, environmental conditions or interference.

### 10.1 The Sensor Selection Problem with Unreliable Sensors

More formally, we imagine every location $v\in V$ is associated with a random variable ${\mathcal{X}}_{v}$ describing the temperature at that location, and there is a joint probability
distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\mathbf{x}}_{V}}\right)}}:=\mathbb{P}\left[{\mathcal{X}}_{V}={\mathbf{x}}_{V}\right]$ that models the correlation between temperature
values. Here, ${\mathcal{X}}_{V}=[{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{X}}_{n}]$ is
the random vector over all temperature values. We follow [Deshpande et al. (2004)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib13) and assume that the joint distribution of the sensors is multivariate Gaussian.
A sensor $v$ can make a noisy observation ${\mathcal{Y}}_{v}={\mathcal{X}}_{v}+\varepsilon_{v}$, where $\varepsilon_{v}$ is zero mean Gaussian noise with known variance $\sigma^{2}$.
If some measurements ${\mathcal{Y}}_{A}={\mathbf{y}}_{A}$ are obtained at a subset of locations, then the
conditional distribution${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\mathbf{x}}_{V}\mid{\mathbf{y}}_{A}}\right)}}:=\mathbb{P}\left[{\mathcal{X}}_{V}={\mathbf{x}}_{V}\mid{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}={\mathbf{y}}_{A}\right]$ allows
predictions at the unobserved locations, e.g., by predicting$\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{X}}_{V}\mid{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}={\mathbf{y}}_{A}]$ (which minimizes the mean squared error).
Furthermore, this conditional distribution quantifies the *uncertainty* in the prediction: Intuitively, we would like to
select sensors that minimize the predictive uncertainty. One way to
quantify the predictive uncertainty is to use the remaining
Shannon entropy

|  | $\mathbb{H}\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}\mid{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}={\mathbf{y}}_{A}}\right):=\mathbb{E}\left[-\log_{2}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}\mid{\mathbf{y}}_{A}}\right)}}}\right)\right].$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

We would like to adaptively select $k$ sensors, to maximize the expected reduction in Shannon entropy (*c.f.* [Sebastiani and Wynn (2000)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib61); [Krause and Guestrin (2009a)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib43)). However, in practice, sensors are often unreliable, and might fail to report their measurements. We assume that after selecting a sensor, we find out whether it has failed or not
before deciding which sensor to select next. We suppose that each sensor has an associated probability $p_{\text{fail}}\!\left({v}\right)$ of failure, in which case no reading is reported, and that sensor failures are independent of each other and of the ambient temperature at $v$.
Thus we have an instance of the Stochastic Maximization problem with $E:=V$,$O:=\left\{\text{working},\text{failed}\right\}$, and

|  | $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=\mathbb{H}\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}}\right)-\mathbb{H}\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}\ \mid\ {\mathbf{y}}_{\left\{v\ :\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(v)=\text{working}\right\}}}\right).$ |  | (26) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

For multivariate normal distributions, the entropy is given as

|  | $\mathbb{H}\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}\mid{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}={\mathbf{y}}_{A}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\ln(2\pi e)^{n}\left&#124;\Sigma_{VA}\left(\Sigma_{AA}+\sigma^{2}I\right)^{-1}\Sigma_{AV}\right&#124;,$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

where for sets $A$ and $B$, $\Sigma_{AB}$ denotes the covariance (matrix) between random vectors ${\mathcal{X}}_{A}$ and ${\mathcal{X}}_{B}$. Note that the predictive covariance does not depend on the actual observations ${\mathbf{y}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}$, only on the set $A$ of chosen locations. Thus,

|  | $\mathbb{H}\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}\mid{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}={\mathbf{y}}_{A}}\right)=\mathbb{H}\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}\mid{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}}\right),$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

where as usual,$\mathbb{H}\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}\ \mid\ {\mathcal{Y}}_{A}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{H}\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}\ \mid\ {\mathcal{Y}}_{A}={\mathbf{y}}_{A}}\right)\right]$.
As [Krause and Guestrin (2005)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib41) show, the function

|  | $g(A):=\mathbb{I}\left({{{\mathcal{X}}_{V}};{{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}}}\right)=\mathbb{H}\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}}\right)-\mathbb{H}\left({{\mathcal{X}}_{V}\mid{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}}\right)$ |  | (27) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

is monotone submodular, whenever the observations ${\mathcal{Y}}_{V}$ are conditionally independent given ${\mathcal{X}}_{V}$.

This insight allows us to apply the result of
§[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") to show that the objective $f$ defined in Eq. ([26](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10.E26 "In 10.1 The Sensor Selection Problem with Unreliable Sensors ‣ 10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"))
is adaptive monotone submodular, using$\hat{f}(S):=g(\left\{v:(v,\text{working})\in S\right\})$ for
any $S\subseteq E\times O$.

#### 10.1.1 Data and Experimental Setup

Our first data set consists of temperature measurements from the network of $46$ sensors deployed at Intel Research Berkeley, which were sampled at $30$ second intervals for $5$ consecutive days (starting Feb. $28^{\text{th}}$, $2004$). We define our objective function with respect to the empirical covariance estimated from the data.

We also use data from traffic sensors deployed along the highway I-$880$ South in California. We use traffic speed data for all working days from $6$ AM to $11$ AM for one month, from $357$ sensors. The goal is to predict the speed on all $357$ road segments. We again estimate the empirical covariance matrix.

#### 10.1.2 The Benefits of Lazy Evaluation

For both data sets, we run the adaptive greedy algorithm, using both the
naive implementation (Algorithm [1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#algorithm1 "In 4.2 Approximate Greedy Selection ‣ 4 The Adaptive Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) and the accelerated version
using lazy evaluations (Algorithm [2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#algorithm2 "In 4.4 Lazy Evaluations and the Accelerated Adaptive Greedy Algorithm ‣ 4 The Adaptive Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")). We vary the
probability of sensor failure,
and evaluate the execution time and the number of evaluations of the function $g$ (defined in
Eq. ([27](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10.E27 "In 10.1 The Sensor Selection Problem with Unreliable Sensors ‣ 10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"))) each algorithm makes.
Figures [5(a)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10.F5.sf1 "In Figure 5 ‣ 10.1.3 The Benefits of the Data Dependent Bound ‣ 10.1 The Sensor Selection Problem with Unreliable Sensors ‣ 10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") and [5(b)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10.F5.sf2 "In Figure 5 ‣ 10.1.3 The Benefits of the Data Dependent Bound ‣ 10.1 The Sensor Selection Problem with Unreliable Sensors ‣ 10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") plot
execution time given a $50\%$ sensor failure rate, on a computer with a$2.26$ GHz dual core processor and $4$ GB RAM.
In these applications, function evaluations are the bottleneck in the computation, so the
number of them serves as a machine-independent proxy for the running time.
Figures [5(c)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10.F5.sf3 "In Figure 5 ‣ 10.1.3 The Benefits of the Data Dependent Bound ‣ 10.1 The Sensor Selection Problem with Unreliable Sensors ‣ 10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") and [5(d)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10.F5.sf4 "In Figure 5 ‣ 10.1.3 The Benefits of the Data Dependent Bound ‣ 10.1 The Sensor Selection Problem with Unreliable Sensors ‣ 10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") show the
performance ratio in terms of this proxy.
On the temperature data set, lazy evaluations speed up the computation
by a factor of between roughly $3.5$ and $7$, depending on the failure
probability. On the larger traffic data set, we obtain speedup factors
between $30$ and $38$.
We find that the benefit of the lazy evaluations
increases with the problem size and with the
failure probability.
The dependence on problem size must ultimately be explained in terms
of structural properties of the instances,
which also benefit the nonadaptive accelerated greedy algorithm.
The dependence on failure probability has a simpler explanation.
Note that in these applications, if the accelerated greedy algorithm selects $v$, which then
fails, then it does not need to make any additional function evaluations to select the
next sensor. Contrast this with the naive greedy algorithm, which
makes a function evaluation for each sensor that has not been selected
so far.

#### 10.1.3 The Benefits of the Data Dependent Bound

While adaptive submodularity allows us to prove worst-case performance
guarantees for the adaptive greedy algorithm, in many practical
applications it can be expected that these bounds are quite loose.
For our sensor selection application, we use the data dependent
bounds of Lemma [6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem6 "Lemma 6 (The Adaptive Data Dependent Bound) ‣ 5.1.1 Data Dependent Bounds ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") to compute an upper bound$\beta_{\text{avg}}$ on $\max_{\pi}f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{k}]})$ as described below,
and compare it with the performance guarantee
of Theorem [5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 5 ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").
For the accelerated greedy algorithm, we use the upper bounds on the
marginal benefits stored in the priority queue instead of recomputing
the marginal benefits, and thus expect somewhat looser bounds. We find
that for our application, the bounds are tighter than the worst
case bounds. We also find that the “lazy” data dependent bounds are
almost as tight as the “eager” bounds using the
eagerly recomputed marginal benefits $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ for the latest and
greatest ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, though the former have slightly higher variance.
Figures [5(e)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10.F5.sf5 "In Figure 5 ‣ 10.1.3 The Benefits of the Data Dependent Bound ‣ 10.1 The Sensor Selection Problem with Unreliable Sensors ‣ 10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") and [5(f)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10.F5.sf6 "In Figure 5 ‣ 10.1.3 The Benefits of the Data Dependent Bound ‣ 10.1 The Sensor Selection Problem with Unreliable Sensors ‣ 10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") show the
performance of the greedy algorithm as well as the three bounds on the
optimal value.

Two subtleties arise when using the data-dependent bounds
to bound $\max_{\pi}f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{k}]})$.
The first is that Lemma [6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem6 "Lemma 6 (The Adaptive Data Dependent Bound) ‣ 5.1.1 Data Dependent Bounds ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") tells us that$\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\ \leq\ \max_{A\subseteq E,&#124;A&#124;\leq k}\ \sum_{e\in A}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$, whereas we would like to bound
the difference between
the optimal reward and
the algorithm’s current expected reward, conditioned on seeing${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, i.e., $\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$.
However, in our applications $f$ is strongly adaptive monotone, and strong
adaptive monotonicity implies that for any $\pi^{*}$ we have

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\leq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right).$ |  | (28) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Hence, if we let $\textsf{OPT}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}):=\max_{\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi_{[{k}]}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$,
Lemma [6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem6 "Lemma 6 (The Adaptive Data Dependent Bound) ‣ 5.1.1 Data Dependent Bounds ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") implies that

|  | $\textsf{OPT}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]+\max_{A\subseteq E,&#124;A&#124;\leq k}\ \sum_{e\in A}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right).$ |  | (29) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

The second subtlety is that we obtain a sequence of bounds from
Eq. ([29](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S10.E29 "In 10.1.3 The Benefits of the Data Dependent Bound ‣ 10.1 The Sensor Selection Problem with Unreliable Sensors ‣ 10 Experiments ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")).
If we consider the (random) sequence of partial realizations observed by the adaptive
greedy algorithm, $\emptyset={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{0}\subset{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{k}$, we obtain $k+1$ bounds $\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{k}$, where$\beta_{i}:=\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i}\right]+\max_{A\subseteq E,&#124;A&#124;\leq k}\ \sum_{e\in A}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i}}}\right)$.
Taking the expectation over ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}$, note that for any $\pi$, and
any $i$,

|  | $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{k}]})\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\textsf{OPT}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{i})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{i}\right].$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

Therefore for any $0\leq i\leq k$ , $\beta_{i}$ is a random variable
whose *expectation* is an upper bound on the optimal expected reward of
any policy. At this point we may be tempted to use the minimum of
these, i.e., $\beta_{\min}:=\min_{i}\left\{\beta_{i}\right\}$ as our ultimate bound.
However, a collection of random variables $X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}$ with$\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]\geq\tau$ for all $i$ does not, in general, satisfy$\min_{i}\left\{X_{i}\right\}\geq\tau$. While it is possible in our case, with its
independent sensor failures, to use concentration inequalities to bound$\min_{i}\left\{\beta_{i}\right\}-\min_{i}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{i}\right]\right\}$ with high
probability, and thus add an appropriate term to obtain a true upper
bound from $\beta_{\min}$, we take a different approach;
we simply use the average bound$\beta_{\text{avg}}:=\frac{1}{k+1}\sum_{i=0}^{k}\beta_{i}$.
Of course, depending on the application, a particular bound $\beta_{i}$ (chosen independently of the sequence ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{0},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}_{k}$) may be superior.
For example, if $g$ is modular, then $\beta_{0}$ is best, whereas if$g$ exhibits strong diminishing returns, then bounds $\beta_{i}$ with
larger values of $i$ may be significantly tighter.

*(a) *Temperature Data: Execution time (sec) for
the naive vs accelerated implementations of adaptive greedy
vs. the budget $k$ on number of sensors selected,
when $p_{\text{fail}}\!\left({v}\right)=0.5$ for all $v$, plotted with standard errors.**

*(b) *Traffic Data: Execution time (sec) for
the naive vs accelerated implementations of adaptive greedy
vs. the budget $k$ on number of sensors selected,
when $p_{\text{fail}}\!\left({v}\right)=0.5$ for all $v$, plotted with standard errors.**

*(c) *Temperature Data: The ratio of function evaluations
made by the naive vs accelerated implementations of adaptive greedy
vs. the budget $k$ on number of sensors selected, for various
failure rates. Averaged over $100$ runs.**

*(d) *Traffic Data: The ratio of function evaluations
made by the naive vs accelerated implementations of adaptive greedy
vs. the budget $k$ on number of sensors selected, for various
failure rates. Averaged over $10$ runs.**

*(e) *Temperature Data: Rewards & bounds on the optimal value when $p_{\text{fail}}\!\left({v}\right)=0.5$ for
all $v$ vs. the budget $k$ on number of sensors
selected, plotted with standard errors.**

*(f) *Traffic Data: Rewards & bounds on the optimal value when $p_{\text{fail}}\!\left({v}\right)=0.5$ for
all $v$ vs. the budget $k$ on number of sensors
selected, plotted with standard errors.**

## 11 Adaptivity Gap

An important question in adaptive optimization is *how much
better* adaptive policies can perform when compared to non-adaptive
policies. This is quantified by the *adaptivity gap*, which is the
worst-case ratio, over problem instances, of the performance of the optimal adaptive policy to the optimal non-adaptive solution. [Asadpour et al. (2008)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib3) show that in the Stochastic Submodular
Maximization problem with independent failures (as considered in
§[6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S6 "6 Application: Stochastic Submodular Maximization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), the expected value of the
optimal non-adaptive policy is at most a constant factor $1-1/e$ worse
than the expected value of the optimal adaptive policy.
While we currently do not have lower bounds for the adaptivity gap of the
general Adaptive Stochastic Maximization problem ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")),
we can show that even in the case of adaptive submodular functions, the
min-cost cover and min-sum cover versions
have large adaptivity gaps, and thus there
is a large benefit of using adaptive algorithms.
In these cases, the adaptivity gap is defined as the worst-case ratio
of the expected cost of the optimal non-adaptive policy divided by
the expected cost of the optimal adaptive policy.
For the Adaptive Stochastic Minimum Cost Coverage problem ([2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E2 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), [Goemans and Vondrák (2006)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib21) show the special case
of Stochastic Set Coverage without
multiplicities has an adaptivity gap of $\Omega(&#124;E&#124;)$.
Below we exhibit an adaptive stochastic optimization instance with
adaptivity gap of $\Omega(&#124;E&#124;/\log&#124;E&#124;)$ for the
Adaptive Stochastic Min-Sum Cover problem ([3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E3 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), which also happens to
have the same adaptivity gap for
Adaptive Stochastic Minimum Cost Coverage.

###### Theorem 25

Even for adaptive submodular functions, the adaptivity gap of
Adaptive Stochastic Min-Sum Cover
is $\Omega(n/\log n)$, where $n=&#124;E&#124;$.

Proof  Suppose $E=\{1,\dots,n\}$. Consider the active learning
problem where our hypotheses $h:E\rightarrow\left\{-1,1\right\}$ are
threshold functions, i.e., $h(e)=1$ if $e\geq\ell$ and$h(e)=-1$ if $e<\ell$ for some threshold $\ell$. There is a
uniform distribution over thresholds $\ell\in\{1,\dots,n+1\}$. In
order to identify the correct hypothesis with threshold $\ell$, our
policy must observe at least one of $\ell-1$ or $\ell$ (and both
of them if $1<\ell\leq n$).
Let $\hat{\pi}$ be an optimal non-adaptive
policy for this problem.
Note that $\hat{\pi}$ can be represented as a permutation of$E$, because
observing an element multiple times can only increase the cost
while providing no benefit over observing it once,
and each element must eventually be selected to guarantee coverage.
For the min-sum cover objective, consider playing $\hat{\pi}$ for$n/4$ time steps. Then $\mathbb{P}\left[\ell\text{ observed in }n/4\text{ steps}\right]=n/4(n+1)$. Likewise$\mathbb{P}\left[\ell-1\text{ observed in }n/4\text{ steps}\right]=n/4(n+1)$.
Since at least one of these events must occur to identify the correct
hypothesis, by a union bound

|  | $\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{\pi}\text{ identifies the correct hypothesis in }n/4\text{ steps}\right]\quad\leq\quad\frac{n}{2(n+1)}\quad\leq\quad 1/2.$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

Thus a lower bound on the expected cost of $\hat{\pi}$ is $n/8$, since for $n/4$ time
steps a cost of at least $1/2$ is incurred. Thus, for both the min-cost and min-sum cover objectives the cost of the optimal non-adaptive policy is $\Omega(n)$.

As an example adaptive policy, we can implement a natural binary
search strategy, which is guaranteed to identify the correct
hypothesis after $O(\log n)$ steps, thus incurring cost $O(\log n)$,
proving an adaptivity gap of $\Omega(n/\log n)$.

## 12 Hardness of Approximation

In this paper, we have developed the notion of adaptive submodularity,
which characterizes when certain adaptive stochastic optimization
problems are well-behaved in the sense that a simple greedy policy
obtains a constant factor or polylogarithmic factor approximation to the
best policy.

In contrast, we can also show that without adaptive submodularity, the adaptive stochastic
optimization problems ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), ([2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E2 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), and ([3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E3 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) are extremely inapproximable, even with (pointwise) *modular* objective functions (i.e., those where for each ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$ is modular/linear in the first argument):
We cannot hope to achieve an ${\mathcal{O}}(&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon})$ approximation ratio for these problems, unless the polynomial hierarchy
collapses down to $\Sigma^{P}_{2}$.

###### Theorem 26

For all (possibly non-constant) $\beta\geq 1$,
no polynomial time algorithm for Adaptive Stochastic
Maximization with a budget of $\beta k$ items
can approximate the reward of an optimal policy with a budget of only $k$ items
to within a
multiplicative factor of ${\mathcal{O}}(&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon}/\beta)$ for any$\varepsilon>0$, unless $\textrm{PH}=\Sigma^{P}_{2}$. This holds even
for pointwise modular $f$.

We provide the proof of Theorem [26](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem26 "Theorem 26 ‣ 12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") in Appendix [15.7](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS7 "15.7 Proof of Approximation Hardness in the Absence of Adaptive Submodularity ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").
Note that by setting $\beta=1$, we obtain ${\mathcal{O}}(&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon})$ hardness for Adaptive Stochastic Maximization.
It turns out that
in the instance distribution we construct in the
proof of Theorem [26](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem26 "Theorem 26 ‣ 12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") the
optimal policy covers every realization (i.e., always finds the
treasure) using a budget of$k={\mathcal{O}}(&#124;E&#124;^{\varepsilon/2})$ items.
Hence if $\textrm{PH}\neq\Sigma^{P}_{2}$ then
any randomized polynomial time algorithm wishing to cover this
instance must have a budget$\beta=\Omega(&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon})$ times larger than the
optimal policy, in order to ensure the ratio of rewards, which is$\Omega(&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon}/\beta)$, equals one.
This yields the following corollary.

###### Corollary 27

No polynomial time algorithm for Adaptive Stochastic Min
Cost Coverage
can approximate the cost of an optimal policy
to within a multiplicative factor of ${\mathcal{O}}(&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon})$ for any$\varepsilon>0$, unless $\textrm{PH}=\Sigma^{P}_{2}$. This holds even for pointwise modular $f$.

Furthermore, since in the instance distribution we construct the
optimal policy $\pi^{*}$ covers every realization using a budget of $k$, it has$c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi^{*}}\right)\leq k$. Moreover,
since we have shown that under our
complexity theoretic assumptions, any polynomial time randomized
policy $\pi$ with budget $\beta k$ achieves at most$o(\beta/&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon})$ of the (unit) value obtained by the
optimal policy with budget $k$, it follows that$c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right)=\Omega(\beta k)$. Since we require $\beta=\Omega(&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon})$ to cover any set of
realizations constituting, e.g., half of the probability mass, we obtain the following corollary.

###### Corollary 28

No polynomial time algorithm for Adaptive Stochastic Min-Sum Cover
can approximate the cost of an optimal policy
to within a multiplicative factor of ${\mathcal{O}}(&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon})$ for any$\varepsilon>0$, unless $\textrm{PH}=\Sigma^{P}_{2}$. This holds even for pointwise modular $f$.

## 13 Related Work

There is a large literature on adaptive optimization under partial
observability which relates to adaptive submodularity, which can be broadly organized into several different categories. Here, we only review relevant related work that is not already discussed elsewhere in the manuscript.

### 13.1 Adaptive Versions of Classic Non-adaptive Optimization Problems

Many approaches consider stochastic generalizations of specific classic non-adaptive optimization problems, such as Set Cover ([Goemans and Vondrák, 2006](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib21); [Liu et al., 2008](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib47)), Knapsack ([Dean et al., 2008](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib12); [Dean et al., 2005](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib11)) and Traveling Salesman ([Gupta et al., 2010](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib36)). In contrast, in this paper our goal is to introduce a general problem structure – adaptive submodularity – that unifies a number of adaptive optimization problems. This is similar to how the classic notion of submodularity unifies various optimization problems such as Set Cover, Facility Location, nonadaptive Bayesian Experimental Design, etc.

### 13.2 Competitive Online Optimization

Another active area of
research in sequential optimization is the study of *competitive
online algorithms*.
A particularly relevant example is Online Set Cover [Alon et al. (2009)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib1),
where there is a known set system, an arbitrary sequence of elements
is presented to the algorithm, and the algorithm must irrevocably
select sets to purchase such that at all times the purchased sets
cover all elements which have appeared so far. [Alon et al. (2009)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib1) obtain a
polylogarithmic approximation to this problem, via an online
primal–dual framework which has been profitably applied to many other
problems. [Buchbinder and Naor (2009)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib6) provide a detailed treatment of this
framework. Note that competitive analysis focuses on worst–case
scenarios. In contrast, we assume probabilistic information about the
world and optimize for the average case.

### 13.3 (Noisy) Interactive Submodular Set Cover

Recent work
by [Guillory and Bilmes (2010)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib33); [Guillory and Bilmes (2011)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib34) considers a class of adaptive
optimization problems over a family of monotone submodular objectives $\left\{f_{h}:h\in H\right\}$.
In their problem, one must cover a monotone submodular objective$f_{h^{*}}$ which
depends on the (initially unknown) target hypothesis $h^{*}\in H$, by adaptively
issuing queries and getting responses. Unlike traditional pool-based
active learning, each query may generate a response from a *set* of
valid responses depending on the target hypothesis.
The reward is calculated by evaluating $f_{h^{*}}$ on the set of (query, response)
pairs observed, and the goal is to obtain some threshold $Q$ of
objective value at minimum total query cost, where queries may have
nonuniform costs.
In the noisy variant of the problem ([Guillory and Bilmes, 2011](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib34)), the set of (query, response)
pairs observed need not be consistent with any hypothesis in $H$, and the goal
is to obtain $Q$ of value for all hypotheses that are “close”
to being consistent with the observations.
For both variants, [Guillory and Bilmes](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib34) consider the worst-case policy cost, and
provide greedy algorithms optimizing clever hybrid objective
functions.
They prove an approximation guarantee of $\ln(Q&#124;H&#124;)+1$ for
integer valued objective functions $\left\{f_{h}\right\}_{h\in H}$ in
the noise–free case, and similar logarithmic approximation guarantees
for the noisy case.

While similar in spirit to this work, there are several significant
differences between the two.
Guillory and Bilmes focus on worst-case
policy cost, while we focus mainly on average-case policy cost.
The structure of adaptive submodularity depends on the prior ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$, whereas
there is no such dependence
in Interactive Submodular Set Cover.
This dependence in turn allows us to obtain results, such as
Theorem [13](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem13 "Theorem 13 ‣ 5.2.1 Minimizing the Average Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") for self–certifying instances,
whose approximation guarantee does not depend on the number of
realizations in the way that the guarantees for Interactive
Submodular Set Cover depend on $&#124;H&#124;$. As
Guillory and Bilmes
prove, the latter dependence
is fundamental under reasonable complexity-theoretic assumptions66 6 They
reduce to Set Cover and use the result of [Feige (1998)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib14),
which requires the assumption$\textrm{NP}\nsubseteq\text{DTIME}(n^{{\mathcal{O}}(\log\log n)})$, but it suffices to
assume only $\textrm{P}\neq\textrm{NP}$ using the Set Cover approximation hardness
result of [Raz and Safra (1997)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib58) instead..
An interesting open problem within the adaptive submodularity framework
that is highlighted by the work on Interactive Submodular Set Cover
is to identify useful instance-specific properties that
are sufficient to improve upon the worst-case approximation guarantee of
Theorem [14](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem14 "Theorem 14 ‣ 5.2.2 Minimizing the Worst-Case Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

### 13.4 Greedy Frameworks for Adaptive Optimization

The paper
that is perhaps
closest in spirit to this work is the one on Stochastic Depletion problems by [Chan and Farias (2009)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib7), who also identify a general class of adaptive optimization problems than can be near-optimally solved using greedy algorithms (which in their setting give a factor 2 approximation).
However, the similarity is mainly on a conceptual level: The problems and approaches, as well as example applications considered, are quite different.

### 13.5 Stochastic Optimization with Recourse

A class of adaptive
optimization problems studied extensively in operations research
since [Dantzig (1955)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib9) is the area of *stochastic optimization with recourse*. Here, an optimization problem, such as Set Cover, Steiner Tree or Facility Location, is presented in multiple stages. At each stage, more information is revealed, but costs of actions increase. A key difference to the problems studied in this paper is that in these problems, information gets revealed independently of the actions taken by the algorithm. There are general efficient, sampling based (approximate) reductions of multi-stage optimization to the deterministic setting; see, e.g.,[Gupta et al. (2005)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib35).

### 13.6 Bayesian Global Optimization

Adaptive Stochastic Optimization is also related to the problem of
Bayesian Global Optimization (for a recent
survey of the area, *c.f.* [Brochu et al. (2009)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib5)). In Bayesian Global Optimization, the goal is to
adaptively select inputs in order to maximize an unknown function that
is expensive to evaluate (and can possibly only be evaluated using
noisy observations). A common approach that has been successful in
many applications (for a recent application in machine learning, *c.f.* [Lizotte et al. (2007)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib48)), is to assume a prior distribution, such as a Gaussian process, over the unknown objective function. Several criteria for selecting inputs have been developed, such as the Expected Improvement ([Jones et al., 1998](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib37)) criterion. However, while recently performance guarantees where obtained in the no-regret setting ([Grünewälder et al., 2010](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib29); [Srinivas et al., 2010](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib63)), we are not aware of any approximation guarantees for Bayesian Global Optimization.

### 13.7 Probabilistic Planning

The problem of decision making
under partial observability has also been extensively studied in
stochastic optimal control. In particular, Partially Observable Markov
Decision Processes ([Smallwood and Sondik, 1973](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib62)), abbreviated as POMDPs, are a general framework that captures many adaptive optimization problems under partial observability. Unfortunately, solving POMDPs is PSPACE hard ([Papadimitriou and Tsitsiklis, 1987](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib56)), thus typically heuristic algorithms with no approximation guarantees are applied ([Pineau et al., 2006](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib57); [Ross et al., 2008](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib59)).
For some special instances of POMDPs related to Multi-armed Bandit
problems, (near-)optimal policies can be found. These include the
(optimal) Gittins-index policy for the classic Multi-armed Bandit problem ([Gittins and Jones, 1979](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib20)) and approximate policies for the Multi-armed Bandit problem with metric switching costs ([Guha and Munagala, 2009](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib30)) and special cases of the Restless Bandit problem ([Guha et al., 2009](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib31)). The problems considered in this paper can be formalized as POMDPs, albeit with exponentially large state space (where the world state represents the selected items and state/outcome of each item). Thus our results can be interpreted as widening the class of partially observable planning problems that can be efficiently approximately solved.

### 13.8 Previous Work by the Authors & Subsequent Developments

This manuscript is an extended version of a paper that appeared in the
Conference on Learning Theory [Golovin and Krause (2010)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib22).
More recently, [Golovin and Krause (2011b)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib24) proved performance
guarantees for the greedy policy for the problem of
maximizing the expected value of a policy under constraints more
complex than simply selecting at most $k$ items.
These include *matroid* constraints, where a policy can only
select independent sets of items and the greedy policy obtains a$1/2$–approximation for adaptive monotone submodular objectives, and
more generally $p$-*independence system* constraints, where the
greedy policy obtains a $1/(p+1)$–approximation. [Golovin et al. (2010)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib26) and, shortly thereafter, [Bellala and Scott (2010)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib4), used the adaptive submodularity framework to obtain the first algorithms
with provable (squared logarithmic) approximation guarantees for the difficult and fundamental problem of
active learning with persistent noise.
Finally, [Golovin et al. (2011)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib27) used adaptive submodularity in the
context of a dynamic conservation planning, and obtain competitiveness guarantees
for an ecological reserve design problem.

## 14 Conclusions

Planning under partial observability is a central but notoriously difficult problem in artificial intelligence.
In this paper, we identified a novel, general class of adaptive optimization problems under uncertainty that are amenable to efficient, greedy (approximate) solution.
In particular, we introduced the concept of *adaptive submodularity*, generalizing submodular set functions to adaptive
policies. Our generalization is based on a natural adaptive analog of the
diminishing returns property well understood for set functions.
In the special case of deterministic
distributions, adaptive submodularity reduces to the classical notion of
submodular set functions. We proved that several guarantees carried by the
non-adaptive greedy algorithm for submodular set functions generalize
to a natural adaptive greedy algorithm in the case of adaptive submodular
functions, for constrained maximization and certain natural
coverage problems with both minimum cost and minimum sum objectives. We also showed how the adaptive greedy algorithm can be accelerated using lazy evaluations, and how one can compute data-dependent bounds on the optimal solution.
We illustrated the usefulness of the concept by
giving several examples of adaptive submodular objectives arising in
diverse AI applications including sensor placement, viral marketing, automated diagnosis and
pool-based active learning. Proving adaptive submodularity for these
problems allowed us to recover existing results in these applications
as special cases and lead to natural generalizations.
Our experiments on real data indicate that adaptive submodularity can
provide practical benefits, such as significant speed ups
and tighter data-dependent bounds.
We believe that our results provide an interesting step in the
direction of exploiting structure to solve complex stochastic optimization and planning problems under partial observability.

## 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs

In this appendix we provide all of the proofs omitted from the main text.
For the results of §[5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S5 "5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"),
we do so by first explaining how our results generalize to the case where items have costs, and then proving generalizations which incorporate item costs.

### 15.1 Incorporating Costs: Preliminaries

In this section we provide the preliminaries required to define and analyze the versions of our problems with non-uniform item costs.
We suppose each item $e\in E$ has a
cost $c(e)$, and the cost of a set$S\subseteq E$ is given by the modular function $c(S)=\sum_{e\in S}c(e)$.
We define the generalizations of problems ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), ([2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E2 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), and ([3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S2.E3 "In 2.3 Adaptive Stochastic Maximization, Coverage, and Min-Sum Coverage ‣ 2 Adaptive Stochastic Optimization ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) in §[15.3](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS3 "15.3 The Max-Cover Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), §[15.4](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS4 "15.4 The Min-Cost-Cover Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), and §[15.5](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.SS5 "15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), respectively.

Our results are with respect to the greedy policy $\pi^{\text{greedy}}$ and $\alpha$-approximate greedy policies. With costs, the greedy policy
selects an item maximizing $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)/c(e)$, where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ is the current partial realization.

###### Definition 29 (Approximate Greedy Policy with Costs)

A policy $\pi$ is an $\alpha$-*approximate greedy policy* if
for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ such that there exists $e\in E$ with $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)>0$,

|  | $\pi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\in\left\{e\ :\ \frac{\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)}{c(e)}\ \geq\ \frac{1}{\alpha}\max_{e^{\prime}}\left({\frac{\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e^{\prime}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)}{c(e^{\prime})}}\right)\right\},$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

and $\pi$ terminates upon observing any ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ such that $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\leq 0$ for all $e\in E$.
That is, an $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy always obtains at least$(1/\alpha)$ of the maximum possible ratio of conditional expected
marginal benefit to cost, and terminates when no more benefit can be
obtained in expectation.
A *greedy policy* is any $1$-approximate greedy policy.

It will be convenient to imagine the policy executing over time, such that
when a policy $\pi$ selects an item $e$, it starts to *run* $e$, and *finishes running* $e$ after$c(e)$ units of time.
We next generalize the definition of policy truncation. Actually we require three such generalizations, which are all equivalent in the unit cost case.

###### Definition 30 (Strict Policy Truncation)

The *strict level $t$ truncation* of a policy $\pi$, denoted by$\pi_{[\leftarrow{t}]}$, is obtained by running $\pi$ for $t$ time units, and
unselecting items whose runs have not finished by time $t$.
Formally, $\pi_{[\leftarrow{t}]}$ has domain$\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\dom(\pi)\ :\ c(\pi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))+\sum_{e\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})}c(e)\leq t\right\}$,
and agrees with $\pi$ everywhere in its domain.

###### Definition 31 (Lax Policy Truncation)

The *lax level $t$ truncation* of a policy $\pi$, denoted by$\pi_{[{t}\to]}$, is obtained by running $\pi$ for $t$ time units, and
selecting the items running at time $t$.
Formally, $\pi_{[{t}\to]}$ has domain$\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\dom(\pi)\ :\ \sum_{e\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})}c(e)<t\right\}$,
and agrees with $\pi$ everywhere in its domain.

###### Definition 32 (Policy Truncation with Costs)

The *level-$t$-truncation* of a policy $\pi$, denoted by$\pi_{[{t}]}$, is a randomized policy obtained by
running $\pi$ for $t$ time units, and
if some item $e$ has been running for $0\leq\tau<c(e)$ time at time $t$,
selecting $e$ independently with probability $\tau/c(e)$.
Formally, $\pi_{[{t}]}$ is a randomized policy that agrees with $\pi$ everywhere in its domain, has$\dom(\pi_{[\leftarrow{t}]})\subseteq\dom(\pi_{[{t}]})\subseteq\dom(\pi_{[{t}\to]})$ with certainty, and includes each ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\dom(\pi_{[{t}\to]})\setminus\dom(\pi_{[\leftarrow{t}]})$ in its domain
independently with probability $\left({t-\sum_{e\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})}c(e)}\right)/c(\pi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))$.

In the proofs that follow, we will need a notion of the conditional
expected cost of a policy, as well as an alternate characterization of
adaptive monotonicity, based on a notion of policy concatenation.
We prove the equivalence of our two adaptive monotonicity conditions in Lemma [36](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem36 "Lemma 36 (Adaptive Monotonicity Equivalence) ‣ 15.1 Incorporating Costs: Preliminaries ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

###### Definition 33 (Conditional Policy Cost)

The *conditional policy cost* of $\pi$ conditioned on${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, denoted $c\left({{\pi}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$, is the expected cost of
the items $\pi$ selects under ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right)}}$. That is,$c\left({{\pi}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right):=\mathbb{E}\left[c(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}))\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$.

###### Definition 34 (Policy Concatenation)

Given two policies $\pi_{1}$ and $\pi_{2}$ define ${\pi_{1}}@{\pi_{2}}$ as the policy obtained by running $\pi_{1}$ to completion, and then running policy $\pi_{2}$ as if from a fresh start, ignoring the information
gathered77 7 Technically, if under any realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ policy $\pi_{2}$ selects an item that $\pi_{1}$ previously selected, then ${\pi_{1}}@{\pi_{2}}$ cannot be written as a function from a set of partial realizations
to $E$, i.e., it is not a policy. This can be amended by
allowing partial realizations to be multisets over elements of $E\times O$, so that, e.g., if $e$ is played
twice
