It is straightforward to show that$\mathbb{E}\left[C_{e}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\cup\left\{(e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e))\right\})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]=c(e)$,
using the basic fact that if event $A$ is the union of
finitely many disjoint events $B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}$ and $X$ is any random
variable, then $\sum_{i}\mathbb{P}\left[B_{i}\mid A\right]\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid B_{i}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid A\right]$. Hence, in expectation this charging scheme charges the
policy exactly ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}$. Moreover, the rate at which it charges$\pi$ upon observing ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and selecting $e=\pi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ is
exactly $c(e)/\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ per unit expected gain.
Let $\theta({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}):=c(\pi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))/\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$.
Under this
charging scheme the policy pays$\theta({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})dx$ to obtain an additional $dx$ expected reward, given it already has $x$, the true realization is ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, and $({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime})\in A_{x}$ for some ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ with${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$.
Let ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}},{x}}\right)$ be the partial realization in the execution trace of $\pi$ under ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ immediately before it obtains $x$ reward in expectation.
Formally ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}},{x}}\right)$ is the maximal element of $\dom(\pi)$ such that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ and $f({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})<x$, where $f({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$ is defined as in
Eq. ([36](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E36 "In Proof of Theorem ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")).

The total alternative cost of $\pi$ under ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ is
then $\int_{x=0}^{Q}\theta({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}},{x}}\right))dx$, and we can compute
the expected cost of $\pi$ as

|  | ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{x=0}^{Q}\theta({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}},{x}}\right))dx\right].$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

where the expectation is taken with respect to ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}$.
We can then obtain the claimed equality by exchanging the integral and the expectation operators:

|  | ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}\ =\ \int_{x=0}^{Q}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}},{x}}\right))\right]dx\ =\ \int_{x=0}^{Q}\theta(x)dx.$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

We can justify this operator exchange in several ways, for example we can apply Tonelli’s theorem since the prices $\theta$ are always non-negative.

Next we consider the worst-case cost.
We generalize Theorem [14](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem14 "Theorem 14 ‣ 5.2.2 Minimizing the Worst-Case Cost ‣ 5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") by
incorporating arbitrary item costs.

###### Theorem 48

Suppose $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is adaptive monotone and adaptive submodular
with respect to ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$, and
let $\eta$ be any value such that$f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})>f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})-\eta$ implies $f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ for all $S$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
Let $\delta=\min_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$ be the minimum probability of
any realization.
Let $\pi_{wc}^{*}$ be the optimal policy
minimizing the worst-case cost ${c_{\text{wc}}{(\cdot)}}$ while guaranteeing that every realization is covered. Let $\pi$ be an $\alpha$-approximate
greedy policy with respect to the item costs.
Finally, let $Q:=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]$ be the
maximum possible expected reward.
Then

|  | ${c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi)}}\leq\alpha\,{c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi_{wc}^{*})}}\,\left({\ln\left({\frac{Q}{\delta\eta}}\right)+1}\right)\mbox{.}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

Proof  Let $\pi$ be an $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy.
Let $k={c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi_{wc}^{*})}}$, let $\ell=\alpha k\ln\left({Q/\delta\eta}\right)$, and apply Theorem [38](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem38 "Theorem 38 ‣ 15.3 The Max-Cover Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") with
these parameters to yield

|  | $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}]})\ >\ \left({1-e^{-\ell/\alpha k}}\right)f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{wc}^{*})\ =\ \left({1-\frac{\delta\eta}{Q}}\right)f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{wc}^{*})\mbox{.}$ |  | (80) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Since $\pi_{wc}^{*}$ covers every realization by assumption, $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{wc}^{*})=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]=Q$, so rearranging terms
of Eq. ([80](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E80 "In Putting it all together ‣ Proof of Theorem ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) yields $Q-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}]})<\delta\eta$.
Since $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}]})\leq f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}\to]})$ by the adaptive monotonicity of $f$,
it
follows that $Q-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}\to]})<\delta\eta$.
By definition of $\delta$ and $\eta$, if some ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$ is
not covered by $\pi_{[{\ell}\to]}$ then$Q-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}\to]})\geq\delta\eta$.
Thus $Q-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}\to]})<\delta\eta$ implies $Q-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{\ell}\to]})=0$, meaning $\pi_{[{\ell}\to]}$ covers
every realization.

We next claim that $\pi_{[{\ell}\to]}$ has worst-case cost at most $\ell+\alpha k$. It is sufficient to show that the final item executed by$\pi_{[{\ell}\to]}$ has cost at most $\alpha k$ for any
realization.
As we will prove, this follows from the facts that $\pi$ is an$\alpha$-approximate greedy policy and $\pi_{wc}^{*}$ covers
every realization at cost at most $k$.
The data dependent bound, Lemma [37](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem37 "Lemma 37 (The Adaptive Data Dependent Bound with Costs) ‣ 15.2 Adaptive Data Dependent Bounds with Costs ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") on page [37](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem37 "Lemma 37 (The Adaptive Data Dependent Bound with Costs) ‣ 15.2 Adaptive Data Dependent Bounds with Costs ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"),
guarantees that

|  | $\max_{e}\left({\frac{\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)}{c(e)}}\right)\geq\frac{\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi_{wc}^{*}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)}{c\left({{\pi_{wc}^{*}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)}\geq\frac{\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi_{wc}^{*}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)}{k}.$ |  | (81) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Suppose ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\dom(\pi)$.
We would like to say that $\max_{e}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\leq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi_{wc}^{*}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$.
Supposing this is true, any item $e$ with cost $c(e)>\alpha k$ must have $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)/c(e)<\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi_{wc}^{*}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)/\alpha k$, and
hence cannot be selected by any $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy
upon observing ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ by Eq. ([81](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E81 "In Putting it all together ‣ Proof of Theorem ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), and thus the
final item executed by $\pi_{[{\ell}\to]}$ has cost at most $\alpha k$ for any
realization. So we next show that $\max_{e}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\leq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi_{wc}^{*}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$.
Towards this end, note that Lemma [49](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem49 "Lemma 49 ‣ Putting it all together ‣ Proof of Theorem ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") implies

|  | $\max_{e}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right].$ |  | (82) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

and to prove $\max_{e}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\leq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi_{wc}^{*}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$ it suffices to
show

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi_{wc}^{*}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}})\cup\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right].$ |  | (83) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Proving Eq. ([83](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E83 "In Putting it all together ‣ Proof of Theorem ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) is quite straightforward if $f$ is *strongly* adaptive monotone. Given that $f$ is only adaptive monotone, it requires some additional effort.
So fix $A\subset E$ and let $\pi_{A}$ be a non-adaptive policy that selects all
items in $A$ in some arbitrary order.
Let $\mathcal{P}:=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}:\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})=A\right\}$.
Apply Lemma [49](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem49 "Lemma 49 ‣ Putting it all together ‣ Proof of Theorem ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") with $\pi^{\prime}={\pi_{A}}@{\pi_{wc}^{*}}$ and any ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\mathcal{P}$ to obtain

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi_{wc}^{*}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}})\cup A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right].$ |  | (84) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Note that $\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi_{wc}^{*}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}})\cup A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]=f_{\text{avg}}({\pi_{A}}@{\pi_{wc}^{*}})\geq f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{wc}^{*})=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]$.
Since we know$\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$,
an averaging argument together with Eq. ([84](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E84 "In Putting it all together ‣ Proof of Theorem ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"))
then implies that for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\mathcal{P}$

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi_{wc}^{*}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}})\cup A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right].$ |  | (85) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Since ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ was an arbitrary partial realization with $\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})=A$, and $A\subseteq E$ was arbitrary,
fix ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\dom(\pi)$ and let $A=\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$.
With these settings, Eq. ([85](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E85 "In Putting it all together ‣ Proof of Theorem ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) implies
Eq. ([83](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E83 "In Putting it all together ‣ Proof of Theorem ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")), and thus $\max_{e}\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\leq\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi_{wc}^{*}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$, and thus an $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy
can never select an item with cost exceeding $\alpha k$, where$k={c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi_{wc}^{*})}}$. Hence${c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi_{[{\ell}\to]})}}-{c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi_{[{\ell}]})}}\leq\alpha k$, and so ${c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi_{[{\ell}\to]})}}\leq\ell+\alpha k$.
This completes the proof.

###### Lemma 49

Fix adaptive monotone submodular objective $f$.
For any policy $\pi$ and any ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\in\dom(\pi)$ we have

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right].$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

Proof  Augment $\pi$ to a new policy $\pi^{\prime}$ as follows.
Run $\pi$ to completion, and let ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ be the partial
realization consisting of all of the states it has observed. If ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$,
then proceed to select all the remaining items in $E$ in any
order. Otherwise, if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\nsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}^{\prime}$ then terminate.
Then

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi^{\prime}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ \mid\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$ |  | (86) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

where the inequality is by repeated application of the adaptive monotonicity of $f$, and the equality
is by construction.

In §[5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S5.SS2 "5.2 The Min Cost Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") we described how the
result of [Feige (1998)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib14) implies that there is
no polynomial time$(1-\epsilon)\ln\left({Q/\eta}\right)$ approximation algorithm for
self–certifying instances of
Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover, unless $\textrm{NP}\subseteq\text{DTIME}(n^{{\mathcal{O}}(\log\log n)})$. Here we show a related result
for general instances.

###### Lemma 50

For every constant $\epsilon>0$, there is no $(1-\epsilon)\ln\left({Q/\delta\eta}\right)$ polynomial time approximation
algorithm for general instances of
Adaptive Stochastic Min Cost Cover, for either the average case
objective ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\cdot)}}$ or the worst-case objective ${c_{\text{wc}}{(\cdot)}}$,
unless $\textrm{NP}\subseteq\text{DTIME}(n^{{\mathcal{O}}(\log\log n)})$.

Proof  We offer a reduction from the Set Cover problem. Fix a Set Cover
instance with ground set $U$ and sets $\left\{S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{m}\right\}\subseteq 2^{U}$ with
unit-cost sets.
Fix $Q,\eta$ and $\delta$ such that $1/\delta$ and $Q/\eta$ are
positive integers, and $\frac{Q}{\delta\eta}=&#124;U&#124;$.
Let $E:=\left\{S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{m}\right\}$, and set the cost of
each item to one.
Partition $U$ into $1/\delta$ disjoint, equally sized subsets$U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{1/\delta}$. Construct a realization${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{i}$ for each $U_{i}$. Let the set of states be $O=\left\{\textsc{null}\right\}$.
Hence ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{i}(e)=\textsc{null}$ for all $i$ and $e$, so that
no knowledge of the true realization is revealed by selecting items.
We use a uniform distribution over
realizations, i.e., ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{i}}\right)}}=\delta$ for all $i$.
Finally, our objective is$f(\mathcal{C},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}_{i}):=&#124;\cup_{S\in\mathcal{C}}(S\cap U_{i})&#124;$, i.e., the number of elements in $U_{i}$ that we cover with sets in$\mathcal{C}$.
Since $&#124;O&#124;=1$, every realization is consistent with every
possible partial realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$. Hence for any ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$, we have$\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\right]=\delta\hat{f}(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}))$, where$\hat{f}(\mathcal{C})=&#124;\cup_{S\in\mathcal{C}}S&#124;$ is the
objective function of the original set cover instance.
Since $\hat{f}$ is submodular, $f$ is adaptive submodular.
Likewise, since $\hat{f}$ is monotone, and $&#124;O&#124;=1$,$f$ is strongly adaptive monotone.
Now, to cover any realization, we must obtain the maximum possible
value for all realizations, which means selecting a collection of sets$\mathcal{C}$ such that $\cup_{S\in\mathcal{C}}S=U$.
Conversely, any $\mathcal{C}$ such that $\cup_{S\in\mathcal{C}}S=U$ clearly covers $f$. Hence this instance of Adaptive Stochastic Min
Cost Cover,
with either the average case
objective ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\cdot)}}$ or the worst-case objective ${c_{\text{wc}}{(\cdot)}}$,
is equivalent to the original Set Cover instance.
Therefore, the result
from [Feige (1998)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib14) implies that there is no polynomial
time algorithm for obtaining a $(1-\epsilon)\ln&#124;U&#124;=(1-\epsilon)\ln\left({Q/\delta\eta}\right)$ approximation for Adaptive Stochastic Min
Cost Cover unless$\textrm{NP}\subseteq\text{DTIME}(n^{{\mathcal{O}}(\log\log n)})$.

### 15.5 The Min-Sum Objective

In this section we prove
Theorem [15](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem15 "Theorem 15 ‣ 5.3.2 The Adaptive Stochastic Min-Sum Cover Problem ‣ 5.3 The Min-Sum Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), which appears on page [15](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem15 "Theorem 15 ‣ 5.3.2 The Adaptive Stochastic Min-Sum Cover Problem ‣ 5.3 The Min-Sum Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"),
in the case where the items have arbitrary costs.
Our proof
resembles the analogous proof of [Streeter and Golovin (2007)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib64) for the non-adaptive min-sum
submodular cover problem, and, like that
proof, ultimately derives from an extremely elegant performance analysis
of the greedy algorithm for min-sum set cover due to [Feige et al. (2004)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib16).

The objective function $c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\cdot}\right)$ generalized to
arbitrary cost items uses the strict truncation88 8 See
Definition [30](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem30 "Definition 30 (Strict Policy Truncation) ‣ 15.1 Incorporating Costs: Preliminaries ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") on page [30](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem30 "Definition 30 (Strict Policy Truncation) ‣ 15.1 Incorporating Costs: Preliminaries ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). $\pi_{[\leftarrow{t}]}$ in place of $\pi_{[{t}]}$ in the unit-cost definition:

|  | $c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right):=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[\leftarrow{t}]})}\right)=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\left({f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})-f(E(\pi_{[\leftarrow{t}]},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})}\right).$ |  | (87) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

We will prove that any $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy $\pi$ achieves a $4\alpha$-approximation for the min-sum objective, i.e.,$c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right)\leq 4\alpha\,c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi^{*}}\right)$ for all policies$\pi^{*}$.
To do so, we require the following lemma.

###### Lemma 51

Fix an $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy $\pi$ for some adaptive monotone submodular function $f$ and let $s_{i}:=\alpha\left({f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{i+1}]})-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{i}]})}\right)$.
For any policy $\pi^{*}$ and nonnegative integers $i$ and $k$, we have $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{*}_{[{k}]})\leq f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]})+k\cdot s_{i}$.

Proof  Fix $\pi,\pi^{*},i$, and $k$. By adaptive monotonicity$f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{*}_{[{k}]})\leq f_{\text{avg}}({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}}@{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}})$.
We next aim to prove

|  | $f_{\text{avg}}({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}}@{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}})\leq f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]})+k\cdot s_{i}$ |  | (88) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

which is sufficient to complete the proof.
Towards this end, fix a partial realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ of the form $\left\{(e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)):e\in E({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})\right\}$ for some ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.
Consider $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$, which equals
the expected marginal benefit of the $\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}$ portion of${\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}}@{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}}$ conditioned on ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
Lemma [37](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem37 "Lemma 37 (The Adaptive Data Dependent Bound with Costs) ‣ 15.2 Adaptive Data Dependent Bounds with Costs ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") allows us to bound it as

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\right]\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\left[c\left({{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\right]\cdot\max_{e}\left({\frac{\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)}{c(e)}}\right),$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

where the expectations are taken
over the internal randomness of $\pi^{*}$, if there is any. Note
that
for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, we have$\mathbb{E}\left[c(E({\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}))\right]\leq k$, where the
expectation is again taken over the internal randomness of $\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}$.
Hence $\mathbb{E}\left[c\left({{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\right]\leq k$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$. It
follows that $\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\right]\leq k\cdot\max_{e}\left({\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)/c(e)}\right)$.
By definition of an $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy, $\pi$ obtains at least

|  | $(1/\alpha)\max_{e}\left({\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)/c(e)}\right)\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)\right]/\alpha k$ |  | (89) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

expected marginal benefit per
unit cost in a step immediately following its observation of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
Next we take an appropriate convex combination of the previous inequality
with different values of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$.
Let ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Psi}}$ be a random partial realization distributed as$\left\{(e,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)):e\in E({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}})\right\}$.
Then taking the expectation of Eq. ([89](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E89 "In 15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) over ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Psi}}$ yields

|  | $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{i+1}]})-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{i}]})\geq\mathbb{E}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\!\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Psi}}}}\right)\right]}{\alpha k}\right]=\frac{f_{\text{avg}}({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}}@{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}})-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]})}{\alpha k}.$ |  | (90) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Multiplying Eq. ([90](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E90 "In 15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"))
by $\alpha k$, and
substituting in $s_{i}=\alpha\left({f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{i+1}]})-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[{i}]})}\right)$, we conclude$ks_{i}\geq f_{\text{avg}}({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}}@{\pi^{*}_{[{k}]}})-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]})$ which immediately yields Eq. ([88](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E88 "In 15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) and concludes the
proof.

Using Lemma [51](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem51 "Lemma 51 ‣ 15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), together with a geometric argument
developed by [Feige et al. (2004)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib16), we now prove
Theorem [15](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem15 "Theorem 15 ‣ 5.3.2 The Adaptive Stochastic Min-Sum Cover Problem ‣ 5.3 The Min-Sum Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

*Figure 6: An illustration of the inequality$\int_{x=0}^{\infty}h(x)dx\geq\sum_{i\geq 0}x_{i}\left({y_{i}-y_{i+1}}\right)$.*

Proof of Theorem [15](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem15 "Theorem 15 ‣ 5.3.2 The Adaptive Stochastic Min-Sum Cover Problem ‣ 5.3 The Min-Sum Cover Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"): Let $Q:=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]$ be the maximum
possible expected reward, where the expectation is taken w.r.t. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}\right)}}$.
Let $\pi$ be an $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy.
Define $R_{i}:=Q-f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[{i}]}}\right)$ and define $P_{i}:=Q-f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}}\right)$.
Let $x_{i}:=\frac{P_{i}}{2s_{i}}$, let $y_{i}:=\frac{R_{i}}{2}$, and
let $h(x):=Q-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{*}_{[{x}]})$.
We claim $f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}}\right)\leq f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[{i}]}}\right)$ and so$P_{i}\geq R_{i}$.
This clearly holds if $\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}$ is the empty policy, and
otherwise $\pi$ can always select an item
that contributes zero marginal benefit, namely an item
it has already played previously. Hence an $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy$\pi$ can never select items with negative expected marginal
benefit, and so$f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}}\right)\leq f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[{i}]}}\right)$.
By Lemma [51](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem51 "Lemma 51 ‣ 15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"), $f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi^{*}_{[{x_{i}}]}}\right)\leq f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}}\right)+x_{i}s_{i}$.
Therefore

|  | $h(x_{i})\geq Q-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]})-x_{i}\cdot s_{i}=P_{i}-\frac{P_{i}}{2}\geq\frac{R_{i}}{2}=y_{i}$ |  | (91) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

For similar reasons that $f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[\leftarrow{i}]}}\right)\leq f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[{i}]}}\right)$, we have$f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[{i-1}]}}\right)\leq f_{\text{avg}}\left({\pi_{[{i}]}}\right)$, and
so the sequence $\langle y_{1},y_{2},\ldots\rangle$ is non-increasing.
The adaptive monotonicity and adaptive submodularity of $f$ imply that $h(x)$ is
non-increasing. Informally, this is because otherwise, if$f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{*}_{[{x}]})>f_{\text{avg}}(\pi^{*}_{[{x+1}]})$ for some$x$, then the optimal policy must be sacrificing immediate rewards at
time $x$ in exchange for greater returns later, and it can be shown
that if such a strategy is optimal, then adaptive submodularity cannot hold.
Eq. ([91](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.E91 "In 15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")) and the monotonicity of $h$ and $i\mapsto y_{i}$ imply that $\int_{x=0}^{\infty}h(x)dx\geq\sum_{i\geq 0}x_{i}\left({y_{i}-y_{i+1}}\right)$ (see Figure [6](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S15.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 15.5 The Min-Sum Objective ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization")). The left hand side is a lower bound for$c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi^{*}}\right)$, and because $s_{i}=\alpha\left({R_{i}-R_{i+1}}\right)$ the right hand side simplifies to$\frac{1}{4\alpha}\sum_{i\geq 0}P_{i}=\frac{1}{4\alpha}c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right)$, proving $c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right)\leq 4\alpha\cdot c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi^{*}}\right)$. $\blacksquare$

### 15.6 A Symbol Table

| $E$, $e\in E$ | Ground set of items, and an individual item.                                                                                                                                                             |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| $O$, $o\in O$ | States an item may be in, or outcomes of selecting an item, and an individual state/outcome.                                                                                                             |
| ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$                                 | A realization, i.e., a function from items to states.                                                                                                                                                    |
| ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$                                 | A partial realization, typically encoding the current set of observations;                                                                                                                               |
|                                                                | each ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\subset E\times O$ is a partial mapping from items to states.                                                                                                                           |
| ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Psi}}$                                 | A random realization and a random partial realization, respectively.                                                                                                                                     |
| $\sim$                                 | The consistency relation: ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$ means ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}(e)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)$ for all $e\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$.                                                                    |
| ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p}}$                                 | The probability distribution on realizations.                                                                                                                                                            |
| ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})$                                 | The conditional distribution on realizations: ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{p}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}):=\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$.                                                                                                                            |
| $\pi$                                 | A policy, which maps partial realizations to items.                                                                                                                                                      |
| $E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}})$                                 | The set of all items selected by $\pi$ when run under realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$.                                                                               |
| $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$                                 | The conditional expected marginal benefit of $e$ conditioned on ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$:                                                                               |
|                                                                | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{e}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right):=\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup\left\{e\right\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ &#124;\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$.                                                                                                                                                                          |
| $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$                                 | The conditional expected marginal benefit of policy $\pi$ conditioned on ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}$:                                                                        |
|                                                                | $\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt\left({{\pi}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right):=\mathbb{E}\left[f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}})\cup E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})-f(\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\ &#124;\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$.                                                                                                                                                                          |
| ${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}\!\left[{e}/{o}\right]$                                 | Shorthand for ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\cup\left\{(e,o)\right\}$.                                                                                                                                                            |
| $k$                                 | Budget on the cost of selected item sets.                                                                                                                                                                |
| $\pi_{[{k}]}$                                 | A truncated policy. See Definition [4](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem4 "Definition 4 (Policy Truncation) ‣ 5.1 The Maximum Coverage Objective ‣ 5 Guarantees for the Greedy Policy ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") (unit costs) and Definition [32](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem32 "Definition 32 (Policy Truncation with Costs) ‣ 15.1 Incorporating Costs: Preliminaries ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). |
| $\pi_{[\leftarrow{k}]}$                                 | A strictly truncated policy. See Definition [30](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem30 "Definition 30 (Strict Policy Truncation) ‣ 15.1 Incorporating Costs: Preliminaries ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). |
| $\pi_{[{k}\to]}$                                 | A laxly truncated policy. See Definition [31](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem31 "Definition 31 (Lax Policy Truncation) ‣ 15.1 Incorporating Costs: Preliminaries ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). |
| ${\pi}@{\pi^{\prime}}$                                 | Policies $\pi$ and $\pi^{\prime}$ concatenated together. See Definition [34](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem34 "Definition 34 (Policy Concatenation) ‣ 15.1 Incorporating Costs: Preliminaries ‣ 15 Additional Proofs and Incorporating Item Costs ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"). |
| $f$                                 | An objective function, of type $f:2^{E}\times O^{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ unless stated otherwise.                                                                                                                   |
| $f_{\text{avg}}$                                 | Average benefit: $f_{\text{avg}}(\pi):=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]$.                                                                                                                                                         |
| $c$                                 | Item costs $c:E\to\mathbb{N}$. Extended to sets via $c(S):=\sum_{e\in S}c(e)$.                                                                                                          |
| ${c_{\text{avg}}}$                                 | Average cost of a policy: ${c_{\text{avg}}{(\pi)}}:=\mathbb{E}\left[c(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}))\right]$.                                                                                                                                                |
| ${c_{\text{wc}}}$                                 | Worst-case cost of a policy: ${c_{\text{wc}}{(\pi)}}:=\max_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}{c(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}}))}$.                                                                                                                                             |
| $c_{\Sigma}$                                 | Min-sum cost of a policy: $c_{\Sigma}\!\left({\pi}\right):=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]-f_{\text{avg}}(\pi_{[\leftarrow{t}]})}\right).$                                                                                                                                                 |
| $c\left({{\pi}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right)$                                 | Conditional average policy cost: $c\left({{\pi}\!\mid\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}}}\right):=\mathbb{E}\left[c(E({\pi},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}}))\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\psi}}\right]$.                                                                                                                                         |
| $\alpha$                                 | Approximation factor for greedy optimization in an $\alpha$-approximate greedy policy.                                                                                             |
| $Q$                                 | Benefit quota. Often $Q=\mathbb{E}\left[f(E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}})\right]$.                                                                                                                                                     |
| $\eta$                                 | Coverage gap: $\eta=\sup\left\{\eta^{\prime}:f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})>Q-\eta^{\prime}\text{ implies }f(S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})\geq Q\text{ for all }S,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}\right\}$.                                                                                                                                                            |
| $\mathbf{1}_{P}$                                 | The indicator for proposition $P$, which equals one if $P$ is true and zero if $P$ is false.                            |

*Table 2: Important symbols and notations used in this article*

### 15.7 Proof of Approximation Hardness in the Absence of Adaptive Submodularity

We now provide the proof of Theorem [26](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem26 "Theorem 26 ‣ 12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") whose statement
appears on page [26](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem26 "Theorem 26 ‣ 12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization") in §[12](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#S12 "12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization").

Proof of Theorem [26](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#Thmtheorem26 "Theorem 26 ‣ 12 Hardness of Approximation ‣ Adaptive Submodularity: Theory and Applications in Active Learning and Stochastic Optimization"): We construct a hard instance based on the following
intuition. We make the algorithm go “treasure hunting”. There is a
set of $t$ locations $\left\{0,1,,\ldots,t-1\right\}$, there is a treasure
at one of these locations, and the algorithm gets unit reward if it
finds it, and zero reward otherwise. There are $m$ “maps,”
each consisting of a cluster of $s$ bits, and each
purporting to indicate where the treasure is, and each map is stored
in a (weak) secret-sharing way, so that querying few bits of a map
reveals nothing about where it says the treasure is.
Moreover, all but one of the maps are *fake*, and there is a
puzzle indicating which map is the correct one
indicating the treasure’s location. Formally, a fake map is one
which is probabilistically independent of the location of the treasure,
conditioned on the puzzle.

Our instance will have three types of items, $E=E_{T}\uplus E_{M}\uplus E_{P}$, where$&#124;E_{T}&#124;=t$ encodes where the treasure is,$&#124;E_{M}&#124;=ms$ encodes the maps, and$&#124;E_{P}&#124;=n^{3}$ encodes the puzzle, where$m,t,s$ and $n$ are specified below.
All outcomes are binary, so that $O=\{0,1\}$, and we
identify items with bit indices. Accordingly we say that${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)$ is the value of bit $e$.
For all $e\in E_{M}\cup E_{P}$, $\mathbb{P}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(e)=1\right]=.5$ independently. The conditional distribution of${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(E_{T})$ given ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Phi}}(E_{M}\cup E_{P})$ will be deterministic as specified below. Our objective function $f$ is linear, and defined as follows:

|  | $f(A,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})=&#124;\{e\in A\cap E_{T}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)=1\}&#124;.$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

We now describe the puzzle, which is to compute$i(P):=(\operatorname{perm}(P)\mod p)\mod 2^{\ell}$ for a suitable random matrix $P$,
and suitable prime $p$ and integer $\ell$, where$\operatorname{perm}(P)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}P_{i\sigma(i)}$ is the permanent of $P$.
We exploit Theorem $1.9$ of [Feige and Lund (1997)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib15) in which they show that if there exist constants$\eta,\delta>0$ such that a randomized polynomial time algorithm can
compute $(\operatorname{perm}(P)\mod p)\mod 2^{\ell}$ correctly with probability$2^{-\ell}(1+1/n^{\eta})$, where $P$ is drawn uniformly at random from$\left\{0,1,2,\ldots,p-1\right\}^{n\times n}$, $p$ is any prime
superpolynomial in $n$, and $\ell\leq p\left({\frac{1}{2}-\delta}\right)$,
then $\textrm{PH}=\textrm{AM}=\Sigma^{P}_{2}$.
To encode the puzzle,
we fix a prime $p\in[2^{n-2},2^{n-1}]$ and use the $n^{3}$ bits of${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(E_{P})$ to sample $P=P({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ (nearly) uniformly at random from$\left\{0,1,2,\ldots,p-1\right\}^{n\times n}$ as follows.
For a matrix $P\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}$, we let $\operatorname{rep}(P):=\sum_{ij}P_{ij}\cdot p^{(i-1)n+(j-1)}$ define a base $p$ representation of $P$.
Note $\operatorname{rep}(\cdot)$ is one-to-one for $n\times n$ matrices with entries in$\mathbb{Z}_{p}$, so we can define its inverse$\operatorname{rep}^{-1}(\cdot)$.
The encoding $P({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ interprets the bits ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(E_{P})$ as
an integer $x$ in $[2^{n^{3}}]$, and computes $y=x\mod(p^{n^{2}})$.
If $x\leq\left\lfloor 2^{n^{3}}/p^{n^{2}}\right\rfloor p^{n^{2}}$, then $P=\operatorname{rep}^{-1}(y)$.
Otherwise, $P$ is the all zero matrix.
This latter event occurs with probability at most $p^{n^{2}}/2^{n^{3}}\leq 2^{-n^{2}}$, and in this case we simply suppose the
algorithm under consideration finds the treasure and so gets unit reward.
This adds $2^{-n^{2}}$ to its expected reward.
So let us assume from now on that $P$ is drawn uniformly at random.

Next we consider the maps.
Partition $E_{M}=\biguplus_{i=1}^{m}M_{i}$ into $m$ maps $M_{i}$, each consisting of $s$ items.
For each map $M_{i}$, partition
its items into $s/\log_{2}t$ groups of $\log_{2}t$ bits each, so that the bits of each group encode a$\log_{2}t$ bit binary string.
Let $v_{i}\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,t-1\right\}$ be the XOR of these $s/\log_{2}t$ binary strings, interpreted as an integer (using any fixed encoding).
We say$M_{i}$ *points to* $v_{i}$ as the location of the treasure.
A priori, each $v_{i}$ is uniformly distributed in $\{0,...,t-1\}$. For a
particular realization of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(E_{P}\cup E_{M})$,
define $v({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}):=v_{i(P({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}))}$. We set $v({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ to be the location of the
treasure under realization ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}$, i.e., we label $E_{T}=\left\{e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{t-1}\right\}$ and ensure${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e_{j})=1$ if $j=v_{i(P({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}))}$, and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(e)=0$ for
all other $e\in E_{T}$.
Note the random variable $v=v({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}})$ is distributed
uniformly at random in $\{0,1,\dots,t-1\}$. Note that this still holds
if we condition on the realizations of any set of $s/\log_{2}t-1$ items in a map, because in this case there is still at least one group
whose bits remain completely unobserved.

Now consider the optimal policy with a budget of $k=n^{3}+s+1$ items to pick.
Clearly, its reward can be at most $1$. However, given a budget of $k$,
a computationally unconstrained policy can exhaustively sample$E_{P}$, solve the puzzle (i.e., compute $i(P)$), read the
correct map (i.e., exhaustively sample $M_{i(P)}$), decode the map
(i.e., compute $v=v_{i(P)}$), and get the treasure (i.e., pick$e_{v}$) thereby obtaining a reward of one.

Now we give an upper bound on the expected reward $R$ of any randomized
polynomial time algorithm $\mathcal{A}$ with a budget of $\beta k$ items,
assuming $\Sigma^{P}_{2}\neq\textrm{PH}$.
Fix a small constant $\gamma>0$, and set $s=n^{3}$ and $m=t=n^{1/\gamma}$.
We suppose we give $\mathcal{A}$ the realizations ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\phi}}(E_{M})$ for free.
We also replace its budget of $\beta k$ items with a
budget of $\beta k$ specifically for map items in $E_{M}$ and
an additional budget of $\beta k$ specifically for the treasure locations in$E_{T}$.
Obviously, this can only help it.
As noted, if it selects less than$s/\log_{2}t$ bits from the map $M_{i(P)}$ indicated by $P$, the
distribution over $v_{i(P)}$ conditioned on those realizations is still uniform. Of course,
knowledge of $v_{i}$ for $i\neq i(P)$ is useless for getting reward.
Hence $\mathcal{A}$ can try at most$\beta k\log_{2}(t)/s=o(\beta k)$ maps in an attempt to find $M_{i(P)}$.
Note that if we have a randomized algorithm which given a random $P$ drawn from$\left\{0,1,2,\ldots,p-1\right\}^{n\times n}$ always outputs a set $S$ of
integers of size $\alpha$ such that $\mathbb{P}\left[i(P)\in S\right]\geq q$, then we can use it to
construct a randomized algorithm that, given $P$, outputs an integer $x$ such
that $\mathbb{P}\left[i(P)=x\right]\geq q/\alpha$, simply by running the first algorithm
and then selecting a random item of $S$.
If $\mathcal{A}$ does not find $M_{i(P)}$, the distribution on the treasure’s location
is uniform given its knowledge. Hence it’s budget of$\beta k$ treasure locations can only earn it expected reward at most $\beta k/t$.
Armed with these observations and
Theorem $1.9$ in the work of [Feige and Lund (1997)](https://arxiv.org/html/1003.3967v5#bib.bib15) and our complexity theoretic
assumptions, we infer$\mathbb{E}\left[R\right]\leq o(\beta k)\cdot 2^{-\ell}(1+1/n^{\eta})+\beta k/t+2^{-n^{2}}$.
Since $s=n^{3}$ and $m=t=n^{1/\gamma}$ and $\gamma=\Theta(1)$ and $\eta=1$ and $\ell=\log_{2}m$ and $k=n^{3}+s+1=2n^{3}+1$,
we have

|  | $\mathbb{E}\left[R\right]\leq\frac{\beta k}{t}\left({1+o(1)}\right)=2\beta n^{3-1/\gamma}(1+o(1))\mbox{.}$ |  |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- |

Next note that $&#124;E&#124;=t+ms+n^{3}=n^{3+1/\gamma}(1+o(1))$.
Straightforward algebra shows that in order to ensure$\mathbb{E}\left[R\right]=o(\beta/&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon})$,
it suffices to choose $\gamma\leq\varepsilon/6$. Thus, under our
complexity theoretic assumptions, any polynomial time randomized
algorithm $\mathcal{A}$ with budget $\beta k$ achieves at most$o(\beta/&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon})$ of the value obtained by the
optimal policy with budget $k$, so the approximation ratio is $\omega(&#124;E&#124;^{1-\varepsilon}/\beta)$. $\blacksquare$
